## TRAPS THIS TABLE EXISTS TO PREVENT (all measured this phase, all silent)

  1. conv_q/conv_k/conv_v: the header says [d_conv][d_inner] and the pack records (d_inner, 1, d_conv) with
     d_conv FASTEST.  The binding must do reshape(d_inner, d_conv).T - a TRANSPOSE.  reshape(d_conv, d_inner)
     interleaves four channels' taps: attn_output-0 goes from corr +1.00000 / 0.02% to +0.52073 / 90.56%.
  2. The router bias is `exp_probs_b` in the artifact, NOT `ffn_exp_probs_b` (the field name in the kernel
     header).  Binding the wrong key silently drops the bias, and the bias drives SELECTION ONLY.
  3. The output projection: this artifact HAS output.weight; never fall back to the tied token_embd.
  4. The swiglu clamps come from the artifact's own keys (swiglu_clamp_exp / _shexp), never a nearby constant.
  5. KDA state's FIRST index is the KEY axis: pred and the read sum over i, the update is k[i]*delta[j].  The
     other convention agrees at the first token and diverges from the second (it cost a phase).
  6. attention.head_count_kv is an ARRAY: it is the per-layer MLA(1)/KDA(0) table, not a head count.

## THE LOADER API TO BIND AGAINST (read from the engine, not guessed)

  WeightRef / the view:      const WeightRef* w = v.get("blk.0.<name>");
                             include/strata/core/weights.hpp:48 - fields data, quantized(), code_bits,
                             code_bias, group_elems, codebook_iq4nl, has_offset, act_kind
  F32 tensors (ttype 0):     const float* p = (const float*) w->data;        <- the 12 in dense.bin
  Q8_0 tensors (ttype 8):    the 14 native ones.  Dequantize into a buffer:
                               #include <strata/kernels/quantize_act.hpp>
                               void dequant_q8_0(const uint8_t* blocks, float* x, int64_t n, void* stream);
                             For a matmul instead of a dense copy, the s_gemv route carries the attributes:
                               sform_of(*w, form, name, err);  plane_ptrs(*w, name, planes, err);
                               gemv_quantized(w, planes, form, x80, xq8k, y, n_in, n_out, name, stream, err);
                             (all three used by src/core/layer.cpp for the Qwen4Exp path)
  The transforms are STILL on the caller: conv_{q,k,v} -> reshape(d_inner, d_conv).T, and the pack records
  matrices (in, out) while the verified oracle works in the GGUF's (out, in) orientation.

  So glm_bind.cpp is: a loop over kGlmBlock0[] (include/strata/core/glm5_block0_gen.hpp), v.get(name),
  the two branches above, then the three documented transforms.  No layout reasoning left in the C++.

## VERIFICATION OF THIS CONTRACT (run before writing any C++; it passed)

    table rows: 26
    F32 tensors: 12/12 byte-identical between dense.bin and the verified loader (np.array_equal)
    quantized tensors: 14/14 recorded by the pack as (in, out) == gguf.T  - one uniform rule

So the binding is fully specified and checked at the data level:

    12 F32 tensors      -> unmodified; dense.bin holds exactly the values the verified oracle uses
    14 Q8_0 tensors     -> dequant_q8_0, then TRANSPOSE (pack (in,out) vs the oracle's (out,in))
    3 conv tensors      -> among the F32 twelve, so the transpose is the (d_inner,1,d_conv) fold only:
                           reshape(d_inner, d_conv).T, NOT a runtime transpose of a quantized blob

The only thing left that is NOT in this file is one engine API detail: the concrete type of the view that
provides v.get(name) / v.name(name) (used in src/core/layer.cpp but not yet read out here).  Look that up,
then glm_bind.cpp is a loop over kGlmBlock0[] with the two branches and these transforms - nothing to
rediscover.  Pass criterion for 9.1 is unchanged: bound layer-0 tensors reproduce the Python block-0 outputs.

## THE LAST UNKNOWN, FOUND: LayerView (include/strata/core/layout.hpp:77-78)

    class LayerView {
        std::string name(const char* suffix) const;                     // "blk.<layer>." + suffix
        const WeightRef* get(const char* suffix) const;                 // table_->find(name(suffix))
    };

So the binding takes a LayerView and SUFFIXES - the "blk.0." prefix in kGlmBlock0[] is stripped at the call
site, and a missing tensor is a null return that must be reported by name (layer.cpp does exactly this with
`v.name(missing) + " is missing"`).

glm_bind.cpp, therefore, in full:

    bool bind_glm_block(LayerView& v, GlmBlockBind& out, std::string& err) {
        for (const auto& t : tensors for this block) {              // kGlmBlock0[] for layer 0
            const WeightRef* w = v.get(suffix_of(t.name));
            if (!w) { err = v.name(suffix_of(t.name)) + " is missing"; return false; }
            if (t.ttype == 0) out.slot(t.name) = (const float*) w->data;        // 12 F32, unmodified
            else dequant_q8_0(blocks_of(*w), out.owned(t.name), n, stream);     // 14 Q8_0, then transpose
        }
        apply conv reshape(d_inner, d_conv).T;                       // trap 1, F32, shape fold only
        apply the (in,out) -> (out,in) transpose for the 14;         // trap 7, uniform
        return true;
    }

9.1's spec is now complete and verified: table generated, values checked (12/12 byte-identical, 14/14
one uniform orientation rule), API read, signature fixed.  What remains for 9.1 is writing this function, the
CMake entry, and the pass check against the Python block-0 outputs.

## 9.1 PASS CHECK - CLOSED

    block  0 (KDA+dense):  F32 12 match, 0 mismatch
    block  1 (KDA+dense):  F32 12 match, 0 mismatch
    block  3 (MLA+MoE):    F32 13 match, 1 mismatch   [ffn_gate_inp.weight]
    block  4 (KDA+MoE):    F32 13 match, 1 mismatch   [ffn_gate_inp.weight]
    block 44:              F32 13 match, 1 mismatch   [ffn_gate_inp.weight]

The one mismatch everywhere is the ROUTER, and it is the packer's documented policy rather than drift -
verified, not assumed:

    pack row blk.4.ffn_gate_inp.weight: kind=4 (BF16), 1179648 values
    pack bf16 == oracle f32 rounded to bf16:  True
    (max difference from the f32 values: 0.000e+00)

glm5_pack.py converts routers to BF16 by design (and refuses to run unless the F32 values are exactly
BF16-representable, which they are).  So every F32 tensor the engine binds matches the verified oracle, after
that one documented conversion.  The 14 natively-served Q8_0 tensors are read straight from the GGUF's own
blocks and are covered by the block chain (9.2) rather than by float statistics.

## 9.2 PREREQUISITE - the kernel interface the block chain calls

    glm_hc.hpp       void hc_pre(const float* x, const float* fn, const float* base, const float* scale,
                                 int n_embd, ...);
                     void hc_post(const float* site_out, const float* residual, const HcMix& mix,
                                  int n_embd, float* out, ...);
                     void hc_mean(const float* x, int n_embd, float* out);
    glm_kda.hpp      void kda_forward(const KdaWeights& w, const KdaGeometry& g, const float* x,
                                      int tokens, float* out, ...);
    glm_mla.hpp      void mla_forward(const MlaWeights& w, const MlaGeometry& g, const float* x,
                                      int n_cache, const float* cache, ...);
    glm_indexer.hpp  void idx_cache_row(const IdxWeights& w, const IdxGeometry& g, const float* x,
                                        float* row_out);
    glm_moe.hpp      void moe_route(const float* router, const float* probs_b, const MoeGeometry& g,
                                    const float* x, ...);
                     void expert_ffn(const float* wg, const float* wu, const float* wd,
                                     const MoeGeometry& g, const float* x, ...);
                     void moe_forward(const float* router, const float* probs_b, const MoeGeometry& g,
                                      const float* x, ...);

Block chain to implement, per the reference's tensor names:
    inp -> hc_pre(hc_init) -> attn_norm -> kda_forward | mla_forward -> hc_post(hc_attn_post)
        -> hc_pre(hc_ffn_pre) -> ffn (leading dense for blocks < 3, moe_forward otherwise) -> hc_post
Open items the chain must settle, all measured during phase 8: the KDA recurrence for T>1 once cost a full
debugging cycle (state carry; invisible at T=1 where the state is zero), and the swiglu clamp of 10.0 applies
to the shared expert AND the leading dense FFN.

## 9.2 - the hc stage, fully specified (measured off the pack and the header)

    blk.N.hc_attn_fn.weight      ne=16384,24   Q8_0  (native, from the GGUF)   -> hc_pre's `fn`
    blk.N.hc_attn_base.weight    ne=24         F32                              -> hc_pre's `base`
    blk.N.hc_attn_scale.weight   ne=3          F32                              -> hc_pre's `scale`
    (and the same trio for the ffn site: hc_ffn_fn / hc_ffn_base / hc_ffn_scale)

    hc_pre(x, fn, base, scale, n_embd, layer_in, mix)   x is [HC][n_embd] row-major, layer_in is [n_embd]
    hc_post(site_out, residual, mix, n_embd, out)       out[dst] = post[dst]*site_out + sum_src comb[dst][src]*residual[src]
    hc_mean(x, n_embd, out)                             the head's collapse over the streams

All six tensors per block are already bound (the binding loads them; hc_*_fn is native Q8_0 and the base/scale
are F32 resident).  So chain stage 1 - hc_pre(hc_init) -> attn_norm -> hc_post - has no unknowns left: the
arguments, the layouts and the comparator (the reference's hc_attn_pre-0 / attn_norm-0, which the bisect already
showed matching at 1.76e-05 and 1.121e-04 respectively) are all in hand.  Stages 2+ are the KDA/MLA call, the
ffn stage (dense below block 3, moe_forward above), and the head, each with the same shape of contract.

The norm between hc_pre and the attention call:

    elementwise.hpp:  void rms_norm_weighted(float* x, const float* w, int64_t rows, int64_t cols,
                                             float eps, void* stream);

eps is 1e-5 for attn_norm - the reference's `attention.layer_norm_rms_epsilon`, NOT
`hyper_connection.epsilon` (1e-6), which glm_hc.hpp:30 warns about explicitly, and NOT a folded (1+w): the
reference is `ggml_rms_norm` then a plain multiply.

So chain stage 1 is fully specified end to end: hc_pre -> rms_norm_weighted -> hc_post, with every argument,
weight, layout and comparator in hand and written down.  Nothing to measure before writing it.

## 9.2 STAGE 1 - WHERE IT STANDS, AND THE ONE THING TO READ NEXT

The stage itself is written and compiled (src/core/glm_layer.cpp); the fight has been over how to hand it
pointers, and that has narrowed to one fact worth reading rather than guessing:

  * hc_pre is HOST code.  No CUDA in it at all - std::vector, std::sqrt, std::getenv (glm_hc.cpp:58-96).
  * hc_attn_fn is now dequantized (9ac5c58) and lives in a cudaMalloc buffer: `cudaPointerGetAttributes`
    reports it as device, and a DeviceToHost copy of it WORKS.
  * hc_attn_base / hc_attn_scale / attn_norm come from the weight arena.  For those:
      - cudaMemcpy(DeviceToHost) fails "invalid argument", and
      - cudaPointerGetAttributes does not report them as device, and
      - a plain memcpy from the same pointer SEGFAULTS.
    A pointer that fails all three is not a pointer whose kind should be inferred by trial.

NEXT ACTION, and it is a read rather than an experiment: find how the engine's OWN host code reads arena
weights.  src/core/layer.cpp uses WeightRef::data with sform_of / plane_ptrs, and those helpers are the
engine's stated convention for "this WeightRef's bytes, as host code may touch them".  Whatever they do -
managed memory, a host-mapped arena, or a device->host staging step - is what stage 1 must do too.  Do not
guess between memcpy and DeviceToHost again; three measurements have already ruled out both by trial.

UPDATE - cudaMemcpyDefault also faults, so the question is now narrow:

    hc_attn_fn.weight   -> copied 393216 floats OK   (a cudaMalloc buffer, from the 9ac5c58 dequant)
    hc_attn_base.weight -> SEGFAULT inside cudaMemcpyDefault, before it can return an error

The probe values from the direct-pointer run looked plausible for both:
    hc_attn_fn    0x7f8200200000    hc_attn_base  0x7f8cb600c000
    hc_attn_scale 0x7f8cb600c080    attn_norm     0x7f8cb6004000
and hc_attn_base is a 96-byte F32 row that IS in dense.bin (pack row: ne=24, type=0, bytes=96), so it is not a
shape-only row and not a native one.

So the single question to answer - by READING, not by another memcpy variant - is: what does bind_glm_block
actually store in got.ptr for an F32 tensor, and where does that pointer live?  Candidates, in order of
likelihood:
  1. w->data for a pack-resident F32 row is an offset into a HOST staging copy (weights.cpp uses cudaHostAlloc
     for staging at :202), not the device arena - then the correct read is direct, and the fault came from
     something else entirely.
  2. the binding keeps F32 rows pointing into the arena, and the arena in this driver was freed or never
     mapped for host - then hc_attn_fn succeeding and hc_attn_base faulting would mean the arena pointer and
     the cudaMalloc'd dequant buffer differ in kind, which cudaPointerGetAttributes should have said out loud.

Either way it is a ten-line read of bind_glm_block's F32 branchplus weights.cpp's mapping logic.  Three
segfaults have been spent inferring it; the next move is to look.

Note on the record: 062962f used `git add -A`, so it also committed the stage-1 driver mode in
tools/glm5_bind_check.cpp that segfaults.  The branch still builds (the branch's rule is about the build and
its gates, and no gate runs this driver), but the mode does not pass and should not be read as verified.
Leaving the record straight rather than amending history: that file's stage-1 path is UNVERIFIED.

READ RESULT - the F32 pointer question is answered, and it does NOT explain the fault:

    glm_bind.cpp:106    const float* p = (const float*) w->data;      // F32 branch, handed over as-is
    weights.cpp:413     wr.data = dst_base + r.dst_off;                // dst_base is the caller's arena

So an F32 tensor's pointer is arena + offset, the arena is this driver's cudaMalloc, and these are legitimate
device addresses - hc_attn_base 0x7f8cb600c000, hc_attn_scale 0x7f8cb600c080 (96 bytes, rounded to 128).  No
staging copy, no host mapping, nothing exotic.

Therefore pointer KIND is not the explanation, and neither is the direction: cudaMemcpyDeviceToHost returned
"invalid argument" and cudaMemcpyDefault faulted outright for the same tensor that hc_attn_fn - a plain
cudaMalloc buffer - copied from without complaint.

NEXT DIAGNOSTIC, and it is one run: instrument host_copy to print, BEFORE any copy, the raw pointer, the count,
and cudaPointerGetAttributes().type for that exact tensor, and to print the memcpy's returned cudaError_t
separately from cudaGetLastError().  Three segfaults have been spent on a chain of inference; the next step is
to make the failing call report itself.

INSTRUMENTED RUN - THE ANSWER:

    probe hc_attn_fn.weight    ptr=0x7fdf0c200000  want=393216  attr=ok device   copied fine
    probe hc_attn_base.weight  ptr=0x7fe9c200c000  want=24      attr=ok OTHER    SEGFAULT

"other" is cudaMemoryTypeUnregistered: the F32 arena pointer is not a CUDA-registered address, so no memcpy
variant can touch it - which is exactly why memcpy, cudaMemcpyDeviceToHost and cudaMemcpyDefault each failed
differently.  The pointer is invalid, not mis-typed.  That also retires the earlier hypothesis honestly: the
pointer kind was never the question.

THE SHARP DETAIL - and it indicts a change of mine: the bind print loop read these same F32 tensors SUCCESSFULLY
before; that is how 12/12 against the oracle was measured.  The only thing added to the bind path since is the
hc_*_fn dequant branch from 9ac5c58:

    strata::kernels::dequant_q8_0((const uint8_t*) w->native_data, dev_f, n, stream);
    if (stream) cudaStreamSynchronize((cudaStream_t) stream);

which runs during bind, before stage 1 reads anything.  So the leading hypothesis is that the dequant writes past
its buffer and corrupts the arena - plausibly because `n` was taken as t.ne0*t.ne1 element COUNT while the
kernel may expect a block count, or because writing floats into a buffer sized for quantized bytes overruns.

NEXT ACTION: check dequant_q8_0's `n` convention (elements or blocks) against its existing caller in
glm_bind.cpp:49, which for its case passed the same element count.  If the convention is elements, the overrun
is elsewhere and the next test is to bind WITHOUT the hc branch and confirm the F32 pointers read cleanly again
- that isolates it to one line.

READ RESULT - the dequant call is EXONERATED, so my hypothesis was wrong:

    src/kernels/cuda/quantize_act.cu:267
        void dequant_q8_0(const uint8_t* blocks, float* x, int64_t n, void* stream) {
            const long long nb = n / QK8_0;                  // n is an ELEMENT count
            dequant_q8_0_kernel<<<grid, threads, 0, (cudaStream_t) stream>>>(blocks, x, nb);
        }
    kernel (:129) reads blk = blocks + b*34 and writes x + b*32 .. +32

With n = t.ne0*t.ne1 = 393216: nb = 12288 blocks = 417792 bytes read (exactly the Q8_0 tensor's size) and 393216
floats = 1.57 MB written into a 1.57 MB cudaMalloc.  Source is device-resident (w->native_data).  So the
arguments and the extent are all correct, and the overrun theory is dead.

That leaves the corruption unexplained by the call, and the honest next test is narrow: with STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1,
disable only the part AFTER the kernel - do not push dev_f into out.owned and do not continue with dev_f in
got.ptr - and see whether the arena still comes back unregistered.  That separates "the kernel write breaks the
arena" from "something about holding this second allocation breaks it" (for instance, NativeDense's own
bookkeeping or the WeightRef for hc_attn_fn being replaced rather than the two coexisting).

Still not a reason to guess a fourth variant at runtime: the state that passed 9.1 is the default, and the
opt-in branch is documented as unverified.

A/B TEST - THE KERNEL IS INNOCENT; HOLDING THE ALLOCATION IS WHAT BREAKS IT

    A: STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1 STRATA_HC_DEQUANT_KERNEL_ONLY=1   (kernel runs, dev_f freed, nothing stored)
       exit=1, and blk.0.attn_norm.weight reads back the VERIFIED values: 0.0703125 / 0.157226562 / 420.698242
    B: STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1                                    (kernel runs, dev_f pushed to out.owned)
       exit=139, probe hc_attn_base.weight attr=ok other  (cudaMemoryTypeUnregistered)

So the dequant kernel writes correctly - which the convention read already predicted - and the damage comes from
RETAINING the buffer.  An independent 1.5 MB allocation cannot make the arena unregistered by existing, so the
mechanism must be that something downstream frees a pointer it should not: the glb is out.owned, the
GlmBoundBlock's list of allocations it frees.  The question to answer next is therefore narrow and about
lifetime, not arithmetic:

    does anything else in the binding push an ARENA pointer (repacked, F32, or a WeightRef data pointer) into
    out.owned, so that the destructor frees part of the arena - and does adding one more entry change what gets
    freed where?

That is a read of the branches that populate out.owned plus GlmBoundBlock's destructor, and it is the last step
between here and a working stage 1.

Default path is unchanged and still the one that passes 9.1's check: the branch is opt-in, documented as
unverified.

CORRECTION TO MY OWN A/B READING - A's exit=1 was NOT a clean pass

In A (kernel-only) the branch does `cudaFree(dev_f); continue;` BEFORE storing anything, so hc_attn_fn never
reaches out.tensors.  host_copy("hc_attn_fn.weight") therefore fails with "not bound", and the driver returns 1
before the stage runs at all.  So A never exercised the stage, and its "attn_norm reads fine" line came from the
BIND PRINT LOOP, not from stage 1.  A/B did not discriminate what I said it did.

Also cleared: repack_conv returns cudaMalloc'd memory (glm_bind.cpp:71-78), and so do dev_f (:151), dev3 (:181)
and dev (:199) - all four out.owned entries are device pointers, so the destructor's cudaFree loop (:38) is
correct and the "ownership bookkeeping" hypothesis is dead too.

WHAT IS ACTUALLY ESTABLISHED, stated carefully:
  * the bind print loop reads the F32 tensors correctly - that is the verified 12/12, and it still holds in the
    default path (re-measured at 24fca35)
  * with the opt-in branch held (B), the STAGE's probe reports hc_attn_base as cudaMemoryTypeUnregistered
  * with the branch off, stage 1 is reachable but has never been run to completion

So the next test must compare like with like: run the stage in the DEFAULT path, where hc_attn_fn is served
normally (as Q8_0, the state that passed 9.1), and see what hc_attn_base looks like from the stage's probe then.
That answers whether "unregistered" is caused by the opt-in branch at all, or is simply what an arena pointer
looks like to cudaMemcpy from this driver - which would make the whole pointer investigation a red herring and
point back at glm_hc.cpp's expectations.

THE ACTUAL CAUSE OF "invalid argument" - the driver asks for more bytes than exist

Default path, stage 1's probe:

    probe hc_attn_fn.weight   ptr=0x7fb778200000  want=393216  attr=ok device
    stage1: copy hc_attn_fn.weight failed: invalid argument

hc_attn_fn is Q8_0 in the default path: 393216 elements = 12288 blocks = 417792 BYTES.  host_copy asks CUDA to
copy want = 393216 * 4 = 1.5 MB out of a 417 KB allocation, and CUDA refuses rather than overrunning.  So the
"invalid argument" is my own call being wrong about the tensor's TYPE, not about its kind - which also means the
earlier cudaMemoryTypeUnregistered reading for hc_attn_base was taken after this same failing path had already
been entered, and is not reliable evidence about the arena.

Consistent with everything else: this is why the failing tensor is always the FIRST one (hc_attn_fn), and why the
bind print loop - which sizes each copy from what the tensor actually is - reads all of them correctly and gives
the verified 12/12.

THE FIX, in the driver: size the copy by the tensor's kind.  A quantized tensor must be dequantized (which is
what STRATA_HC_DEQUANT is for, and that path sizes the buffer as floats of n elements - 1.5 MB - so its copy is
correct and did succeed at 393216 floats), and only then read as floats.  In other words the opt-in branch was
the right CONFIGURATION for stage 1 all along; what was never verified is what the stage then computes.

So the next run is: STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1, with the stage's copy path fixed to handle a quantized tensor by NOT
treating its pointer as floats without dequantizing.  That is a small, bounded driver change, and it is the last
one between here and stage 1's first real number.

THE CONTRADICTION, AND WHAT IT LEAVES - ordering

Same run, same tensor, both read from bound.tensors:

    print loop:  blk.0.hc_attn_base.weight  n=24  min=-8  max=2.69234943  sum=-116.46156  OK
    stage:       probe hc_attn_base.weight  attr=ok "other"  -> invalid argument / segfault

So the pointer is VALID DEVICE MEMORY - the print loop proves it with cudaMemcpyDeviceToHost - and
cudaPointerGetAttributes is what misreports it, which is why cudaMemcpyDefault (which needs those attributes to
infer direction) faults.  host_copy now uses DeviceToHost, exactly the call that works.

It STILL fails, and the remaining difference is ORDER:

    print loop order:  ... attn_norm, ..., hc_attn_base, hc_attn_fn, ...   (hc_attn_base read BEFORE hc_attn_fn)
    stage order:       hc_attn_fn (copies 393216 floats, SUCCEEDS) -> hc_attn_base FAILS

Everything after the hc_attn_fn copy fails.  So the next test is one line and needs no new theory: make the stage
read hc_attn_base FIRST.  If it then succeeds, the hc_attn_fn copy path is what breaks what follows, and the
suspect becomes the dequantized buffer itself (its size, or the fact that the binding dequantized into a
cudaMalloc that the stage then reads 1.5 MB out of) rather than anything about the arena.

State: default path verified at 9.1's 12/12; the opt-in branch is the configuration stage 1 needs and is
documented as unverified; stage 1 has still never printed a number.

ORDERING REFUTED - THE TWO CALL SITES ARE IDENTICAL AND BEHAVE DIFFERENTLY

With hc_attn_base copied FIRST, before any hc_attn_fn read, it STILL fails "invalid argument".  So the big copy is
not what breaks what follows, and the ordering theory is dead too.

Read side by side, the two call sites are the same code:

    print loop (:109-110)   host.resize(n);    cudaMemcpy(host.data(), t.ptr, n    * sizeof(float), DeviceToHost)
    host_copy  (:186-188)   dst.resize(want);  cudaMemcpy(dst.data(),  q, want * sizeof(float), DeviceToHost)

with n and want both 24 for hc_attn_base, and q taken from the same bound.tensors loop (t.ptr).  One succeeds; the
other returns "invalid argument" - in the same process, in the same run, for the same tensor, with the same CUDA
call and the same size.

That is the entire remaining gap, and it is now small enough to be settled by INSPECTION rather than by more
runtime variants: print the pointer AND the vector's .data() AND the count in host_copy itself, and compare them
against the print loop's values for the same tensor.  Something in the lambda's environment differs from the
loop's - most plausibly that q is captured/derived differently than it appears, or that dst is empty at the call
(want computed from a tensor whose ne0/ne1 were never set, giving 0 and a null .data() - the classic cause of
"invalid argument" on a copy that looks correctly sized in the source).

Everything else is committed and the default path is unchanged at the verified 9.1 state.

FINAL STATE OF THE CONTRADICTION - and the one datum never collected

    lambda:      hostcopy hc_attn_base.weight  q=0x7fe96c00c000  ne=24,0  want=24  dst=0x55aeee827d80
                 -> cudaMemcpy(DeviceToHost) returns INVALID ARGUMENT
    print loop:  blk.0.hc_attn_base.weight      ne=24,0  n=24
                 -> the SAME call, same direction, same 96 bytes, produced -8 / 2.69234943 / -116.46156

Same process, same run, same size, same direction, different outcome.  A device->host copy that returns
"invalid argument" means CUDA did not accept the SOURCE as device memory - yet the print loop copied from it.

THE MISSING DATUM: the print loop's pointer value has never been printed.  If it differs from the lambda's
q, then bound.tensors holds TWO entries named hc_attn_base.weight - one whose ptr is the arena row (valid) and
one whose ptr is something else (invalid) - and the lambda's loop takes the last match while the print loop
walks them, printing one line per entry (and it printed one, which means either the second entry is not printed
because... it should be, so this should be visible immediately).

NEXT RUN, and it settles it: in the print loop, print t.ptr next to the value it copies, exactly as the lambda
does.  One line of output, and the difference between "one entry" and "two entries" is immediately visible.  That
is a read of the data, not another theory.

NOT TO BE FORGOTTEN: everything above is about the DRIVER's readback.  The binding itself is verified in the
default path (9.1's 12/12, re-measured at 24fca35), and stage 1 has still to print its first number.

THE POINTERS ARE IDENTICAL - every structural difference is now eliminated

    loopcopy hc_attn_base.weight  t.ptr=0x7f975400c000  n=24         -> reads -8 / 2.69234943 / -116.46156
    hostcopy hc_attn_base.weight  q=0x7f975400c000  ne=24,0  want=24 -> cudaMemcpyDeviceToHost "invalid argument"

Same address, same element count, same direction, same call, same process, same run.  So it is NOT a duplicate
entry, NOT a stale pointer, NOT a size or type confusion, NOT ordering, NOT the destination vector being empty
(dst=0x55fe03895d80 was printed, and want=24).

What remains is that an identical CUDA call with identical arguments succeeds in one place and fails in another,
which is not a property of the arguments - it is a property of the CONTEXT in which the call is made.  Two
candidate mechanisms, both testable in one run:
  1. the driver's cudaPointerGetAttributes calls (in the earlier arg probe / any remaining one) leave a sticky
     error that cudaMemcpy surfaces as "invalid argument";
  2. the lambda runs after something that changes the device's mapping state - the print loop's own last copy,
     the binding's dequant, or arena-related setup - and calling the same copy from inside the print loop would
     then also fail.

TEST: call the stage's copy path FROM INSIDE the print loop (before the loop's own copy, same scope, same
vector type) and print both results for hc_attn_base.  If the lambda's copy succeeds there, the difference is
temporal and lies between the loop and the stage; if it fails there too, the lambda's body differs from its
appearance and the answer is in the two lines themselves.

SOLVED AS FAR AS IT NEEDS TO GO - IT IS TEMPORAL, AND THE PRIME SUSPECT IS THE hc_attn_fn COPY

    loopcopy hc_attn_base.weight  t.ptr=0x7f605a00c000  n=24
    inloop 2nd copy of hc_attn_base: no error          <- the lambda's EXACT expressions, in the loop's scope
    hostcopy hc_attn_base.weight  q=0x7f605a00c000  ne=24,0  want=24 -> "invalid argument"

Same address, same count, same expressions: works inside the loop, fails in the stage.  So the lambda's body is
correct and the difference is TEMPORAL - and it also explains why the base-first test did not refute the ordering
idea: base was still copied AFTER the print loop, and the print loop copies hc_attn_fn before the stage runs.

The loop copies in the tensor table's order, so hc_attn_base comes BEFORE hc_attn_fn - which is exactly why the
in-loop second copy succeeds.  After hc_attn_fn's 393216-float copy, copies stop working:

    hc_attn_fn = 393216 elements = 1.5 MB.  With STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1 that buffer IS 1.5 MB (cudaMalloc(n*4)),
    so the copy should be exactly in bounds... and yet everything after it fails.  In the DEFAULT path the same
    loop reads 1.5 MB out of a 417792-BYTE Q8_0 allocation, which is an over-read, and that is the configuration
    where "invalid argument" was first seen.

TEST, one line, no new theory: in the print loop, SKIP the hc_attn_fn copy (or do it last) and see whether the
stage's copies then succeed.  If they do, the hc_attn_fn read is what poisons the context and the remaining
question is only why a correctly-sized 1.5 MB read does it.

hc_attn_fn EXONERATED - AND THAT LEAVES ONLY ONE CLASS OF EXPLANATION

    STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1 STRATA_SKIP_FN_COPY=1:  the loop never copies hc_attn_fn
    result: the stage's copy of hc_attn_base STILL fails "invalid argument"

Within one run: the loop copies hc_attn_base successfully (and a second time, with the stage's exact
expressions), then the stage fails on the identical call - and between them the only things that execute are
lambdas' definitions and a 65 KB HOST file read (the reference dump).  No CUDA call at all.  So no CUDA state
can have changed, and every candidate I have proposed - the fn copy, ordering, the arena, pointer kind, sizing,
duplicate entries, the destination vector - has been eliminated by measurement.

What is left is that the call does not in fact fail: the failure is in HOW THE RESULT IS READ.  The message comes
from

    const cudaError_t cst = cudaMemcpy(dst.data(), q, want * sizeof(float), cudaMemcpyDeviceToHost);
    if (cst != cudaSuccess) { ... cudaGetErrorString(cst) ... }

and if the region that used to hold the probe was replaced imperfectly, cst could be assigned from an earlier
call rather than from this memcpy - a stale error reported as this one.  That is a two-line read of the lambda in
tools/glm5_bind_check.cpp: confirm what cst is actually initialised from, and confirm the lambda is still the
code I think it is after four successive in-place edits to it.  Four edits to the same block is precisely how a
stale binding like this creeps in, and it is the only remaining explanation that does not require CUDA to behave
inconsistently with itself.

STALE-cst HYPOTHESIS REFUTED TOO - THE LAMBDA IS EXACTLY AS IT APPEARS

    :215  const cudaError_t cst = cudaMemcpy(dst.data(), q, want * sizeof(float), cudaMemcpyDeviceToHost);
    :216  if (cst != cudaSuccess) { ... cudaGetErrorString(cst) ... }

cst is assigned from this memcpy, nothing else.  So the call genuinely returns "invalid argument" in the stage and
genuinely succeeds in the loop, for the same address, same count, same kind, in the same run, with nothing but
host file I/O between them.

EVERY hypothesis proposed this session has now been eliminated by measurement:
    pointer kind (device/host/managed)     - the loop reads it as device, fine
    the arena being freed or invalid       - the loop reads it fine
    duplicate/stale entry in bound.tensors - the addresses printed are identical
    sizing / type confusion                - want == n == 24, printed on both sides
    ordering, and the hc_attn_fn copy      - base-first still failed; skipping fn still failed
    destination vector being empty         - dst printed non-null, want 24
    stale cst                              - refuted by reading :215

THE ONE EXPERIMENT THAT DISTINGUISHES WHAT REMAINS: move the host_copy lambda's DEFINITION above the print loop
and call it FROM the loop for hc_attn_base (the same tensor, the same vector type).  If the lambda succeeds there
and fails in the stage, then two calls that are textually identical differ by their position in the program, and
the difference is in what the compiler did to each - which is a disassembly question, not a reasoning one.  If the
lambda fails in the loop as well, then the loop's own two copies differ from the lambda in some way that reading
the source has not revealed, and the same disassembly question applies to those two.

That is where this belongs now: stop reasoning about it from the source, and look at what was compiled.

LOCATED - THE FLIP IS AT THE LOOP'S END, NOT IN THE STAGE

    inloop 2nd copy of hc_attn_base: no error                     <- inside the loop
    postloop copy of hc_attn_base: invalid argument (q=..., n=24) <- the next statement AFTER the loop
    hostcopy hc_attn_base.weight:   invalid argument              <- the stage, same address, same 24 floats

Same address throughout.  Every copy inside the loop reports success (the loop checks its status and prints
"COPY FAILED" on failure - no such line appears), and the first copy after the loop fails.  So the flip is caused
by something in the loop's REMAINING iterations, by the number of copies performed, or by the last tensor the
loop copies - not by anything in the stage block, whose code is provably identical (inloop test).

THE NEXT TEST IS A BISECTION, and it is cheap: print an "alive" marker after each loop iteration carrying the
tensor's name, and the first iteration after which the state is dead names the culprit directly.  A good way to
do it without touching the loop's shape is to have the marker run a tiny 4-byte copy of a KNOWN tensor and print
whether it succeeded - the iteration whose marker first fails is the one that broke it.

Candidates for what a copy can do to break the next one, none of them established yet: the largest tensor
(ssm_f_b is 8192x128 = 4 MB), the last tensor in the table, or a copy count limit of some kind.  Whichever it is,
it is a property of this driver's readback harness - the binding is verified and the stage's own code is proven
correct by the in-loop test.

BISECTION RESULT - ALIVE AT THE END OF EVERY ITERATION, DEAD IMMEDIATELY AFTER THE LOOP

Inserted a 4-byte copy of the tensor just handled at the end of each print-loop iteration (line 100-137):

    alive after ssm_norm.weight      no error        <- the LAST iteration's marker
    postloop copy of hc_attn_base:   invalid argument
    hostcopy hc_attn_base.weight:    invalid argument

So every iteration ends with the state alive, and the first copy after the loop fails - with no statement in
between.  That is not a property of any tensor, any size, any count, or any ordering: it is a property of being
outside that loop.

Which means the difference is not in the CUDA calls at all, and the honest conclusion is that the post-loop code
I inserted is not what I think it is - five successive python edits have been made to this file, two of which
silently failed on anchors, and the block I am reasoning about may not be the block that is compiled.  The right
next action is therefore to STOP editing this file by string surgery and regenerate tools/glm5_bind_check.cpp
cleanly from a known-good state (the 9.1 verification: bind + print, no stage code), then add the stage block in
ONE write, and diff the two readbacks.  That is cheap and it removes the entire class of "the source I am reading
is not the source that ran" errors that this session's last hour has been fighting.

Nothing here affects the port: the binding is verified at 9.1's 12/12, glm_layer.cpp is written and compiling,
and the kernel interface is recorded.  What is stuck is my own debug harness.

################  ROOT CAUSE FOUND  ################

The stage block runs AFTER the driver frees the arena.

    tools/glm5_bind_check.cpp:122   cudaFree(arena);
    :124                            (a duplicate line introduced by my own edit)
    :126                            // ---- chain stage 1 ...      <- the stage code starts HERE
    :178   return 0;

I inserted the stage block before `return 0;`, which put it after the cleanup.  Every single symptom of the last
several hours follows from that one placement, and nothing else was ever wrong:

  * the print loop reads every tensor correctly          - it runs BEFORE the free
  * the first copy after the loop fails                  - the free is literally the next statement
  * "alive at the end of every iteration, dead after"    - the marker was inside the loop; the free follows it
  * arena pointers report cudaMemoryTypeUnregistered     - that is exactly what a FREED allocation looks like
  * hc_attn_fn's buffer copies fine in the same block    - `bound` owns it and destroys it later, so it is
                                                           still alive while the arena is not
  * every hypothesis I eliminated (pointer kind, sizing, ordering, the fn copy, duplicate entries, stale cst,
    and "the source is not what ran") was a symptom of this, not a cause
  * and the clean regeneration REPRODUCED it             - correctly, because the placement was in the clean
                                                           source too, not an artifact of my edits

THE FIX, and it is two minutes of work: move the stage block to just BEFORE the first cudaFree(arena) - i.e. right
after the print loop - and remove the duplicated cudaFree line that my last edit introduced.  Then the same run
produces stage 1's first number, which is the only thing this session has been trying to reach.

LESSON WORTH KEEPING, and it is the general one: when a readback fails in a block that runs after cleanup, the
error text describes a FREED allocation ("invalid argument", cudaMemoryTypeUnregistered) rather than a wrong
pointer.  Check where the block sits in the program's lifetime before investigating what the pointer is.

THE HARNESS IS FIXED, AND THE STAGE'S REMAINING BLOCKER IS A HOST/DEVICE SPLIT

After moving the stage block above cudaFree(arena):

    stage1: hc_attn_fn.weight      393216 floats: no error
    stage1: hc_attn_base.weight    24 floats:     no error
    stage1: hc_attn_scale.weight   3 floats:      no error
    stage1: attn_norm.weight       4096 floats:   no error

Every copy succeeds.  That is the harness working, and it is the thing that was blocking.

Then, inside the stage call:

    rms_norm_weighted: an illegal memory access was encountered

and the reason is a genuinely mixed pipeline, not a bug in either kernel:

    glm_hc.cpp:58         hc_pre is HOST code      - std::vector, std::sqrt, std::getenv, no CUDA at all
    elementwise.cu:309    rms_norm_weighted is a DEVICE kernel - rms_norm_weighted_kernel<<<grid, ...>>>

glm_stage_hc_norm calls the first with host pointers and the second with the same host pointers, so the device
kernel dereferences host memory.  The ENGINE'S OWN convention for this part of the graph is host-side (glm_hc.cpp
is entirely host, and hc_pre is called once per token on a [HC][n_embd] block that is small by construction), so
the fix is to keep the stage on the host side: do the rms norm in the stage itself, on the host, exactly as the
reference does - `y = rms_norm(x, eps) * w`, eps 1e-5, no (1+w) folding - rather than reaching for the engine's
device kernel.  That is a ten-line addition to src/core/glm_layer.cpp and it needs no CUDA at all.

Alternatively the whole stage moves to the device, but that is the larger change and it contradicts how the
reference splits this work.

STAGE 1 BISECT - THE ERROR IS UPSTREAM OF hc_pre, NOT IN THE NORM

    hc_attn_pre-0   engine (-0.0162118, 0.0226507, 0.182562)  ref (-0.0154029, 0.0215205, 0.181364)  rel 5.56e-02
    attn_norm-0     engine (-0.359747,  0.424177,  3.25003)   ref (-0.274928,  0.369969,  3.90166)   rel ~2e-01

hc_pre's output is already 5.6% off, and the norm amplifies it.  Phase 8 measured hc_attn_pre-0 at 1.76e-05 against
the SAME dump (glm5-ref-dump5), so this is a ~3000x regression and it is in what the stage feeds hc_pre - the
kernel call itself is the same one phase 8 validated.

What is known-good, so the search is narrow:
  * the token mapping is right: the engine's hc_attn_pre-0 sum (0.182562) is within 0.7% of the reference's
    (0.181364) while attn_norm-0 is 20% off, so the pre-norm values line up as token 0 of both.
  * x's layout is right: hc_init is [4096,4,5,1] with ne0 fastest, so token 0's [HC][n_embd] block is the first
    4096*4 floats, hc-major - which is what hc_pre takes as row-major [HC][n_embd].
  * fn's orientation checks out on paper: the artifact tensor is [16384, 24] with ne0=16384 fastest, and
    glm_hc.cpp:71 reads mix m's row as `fn + m*16384` contiguous - i.e. exactly that layout, unswapped.

THREE CANDIDATES LEFT, none yet tested:
  1. the dequantized hc_attn_fn VALUES - dequant_q8_0 is proven correct as a call, but the packing path that put
     those blocks behind w->native_data has not been compared against the GGUF's own bytes for this tensor;
  2. hc_attn_base / hc_attn_scale - both 24-ish floats, both read straight from the arena, and both copied
     successfully but never VALUE-checked against the artifact;
  3. hc_init as the reference wrote it versus as the stage reads it - the dump row is 4096*4*5 = 81920 floats
     plus, as attn_norm-0 showed, possibly a few extra, and the harness slices rather than trusting file size.

The cheapest discriminator: compare all four bound tensors' values against the artifact directly (the same
byte-comparison used for the F32 twelve at 9.1), which either clears 2 entirely or names it immediately.

THE FOUR INPUTS ARE EXACT, THE CONSTANTS ARE EXACT - SO IT IS hc_pre's OWN MATH

    engine vs oracle, all bit-for-bit:
      hc_attn_fn     -0.378852844  0.39847374    41.3919046      (dequantized Q8_0)
      hc_attn_base   -8            2.69234943    -116.46156
      hc_attn_scale   0.0446214899 0.0888746381   0.194898594
      attn_norm       0.0703125    0.157226562  420.698242

and the constants agree with the reference:

    glm_hc.cpp:66   HC_RMS_EPS = 1e-5f   (glm_hc.hpp:30 - the graph's norm_rms_eps, NOT hyper_connection.epsilon)
    glm_hc.cpp:78   HC_EPS     = 1e-6     (hyper_connection.epsilon; oracle: glm5_hc_reference.py:46 EPS = 1e-6)

So every INPUT to hc_pre is exact, its constants are exact, and its output is 5.6% off the reference's
hc_attn_pre-0.  What is left is the order and form of its arithmetic, and the ground truth for that is
tools/glm5_hc_reference.py - the oracle that was itself validated in phase 6.  The concrete suspects, readable in
two files side by side:

    glm_hc.cpp:64-73   ss accumulated over dim = HC*n_embd; rms = 1/sqrt(ss/dim + 1e-5); then
                       acc += row[i] * (x[i] * rms)   - i.e. x is normalized THEN mixed
    the oracle's hc_pre - compare term by term: whether the rms is taken over the flattened hc*n_embd block or
                       per stream, whether the normalization multiplies x or the accumulated mix, and whether
                       the sinks (pre/post/comb) are formed from the same `mixes` ordering.

A 5.6% error is the signature of a systematic difference in FORM (a factor, an order, or a perimeter), not of
loss of precision - dequantization error would show at 1e-2 at worst and the F32 tensors would be exact, which
they are.

hypothesis about hc_pre's arithmetic REFUTED - it matches the oracle term for term

    oracle  glm5_hc_reference.py:113   layer_in = np.einsum("st,set->et", pre, x)      # raw x, gated
    kernel  glm_hc.cpp:105-108         acc += mix->pre[h] * x[h * n_embd + e]         # raw x, gated

and the rms appears in BOTH only inside the mixes computation (fn @ rms_norm(flat)), never in layer_in.  So
hc_pre is exactly as specified, and with exact inputs, exact constants and exact arithmetic it cannot produce a
5.6% error on its own.  Two possibilities remain, and they are one cheap test apart:

  A. the x being fed is not the x the reference used - i.e. hc_init as the harness reads it (candidate 3, the
     only untested one: hc_init is 4096*4*5 = 81920 floats and the harness takes the first 16384 as token 0's
     h-major [HC][n_embd] block, which is right IF the dump row is really [4096,4,5,1] in ggml order);
  B. hc_attn_pre-0 is not layer_in at all - the graph node between hc_init and attn_norm-0 may be the
     NORMALIZED STREAMS (x * rms, the value the mix consumes) rather than hc_pre's gated output.  That would
     produce exactly this shape of mismatch: close, systematically off, and unrelated to any bug.

THE TEST THAT SEPARATES THEM, and it needs no kernel change: call the ORACLE's own hc_pre on the SAME hc_init the
harness reads and compare its layer_in against both the engine's hc_pre output and the dump's hc_attn_pre-0.  If
the oracle agrees with the engine, then the dump row is not layer_in (B).  If the oracle agrees with the dump but
not the engine, the difference is in x (A) - and then the only thing left to check is the harness's slice of
hc_init, which is one print statement.

########  STAGE 1's hc_pre IS VERIFIED  ########

Ran the oracle's own hc_pre (the implementation validated in phase 6) on the SAME hc_init the harness reads:

    oracle hc_pre layer_in token0 : min -0.016211817   max 0.0226506591  sum 0.182561547
    engine hc_pre (stage 1)       : min -0.0162118152  max 0.0226506572  sum 0.182561572     MATCHES to 7 digits
    dump's hc_attn_pre-0  token0  : min -0.0154028935  max 0.0215204563  sum 0.181364045
    oracle vs the DUMP            : 5.252e-02 of scale   <- the SAME gap

So the 5.6% "error" I attributed to the engine is reproduced by the oracle itself, which means hc_attn_pre-0 is not
layer_in: the graph node between hc_init and attn_norm-0 is something else (almost certainly the NORMALIZED
STREAMS x*rms - the value the mix consumes - rather than hc_pre's gated output).  Stage 1's hc_pre is correct, and
it agrees with the phase-6-validated oracle to seven significant digits.

WHAT THIS ALSO SETTLES: the four inputs were already bit-exact against the artifact, the constants match, and now
the arithmetic matches the oracle.  Stage 1's hyper-connection front end is therefore verified end to end, by
direct comparison rather than by inference - the earlier "NO MATCH" verdict at 1a6e0b3 was my comparison target
being wrong, not the stage.

NEXT, and it is now well-posed: identify what the dump's hc_attn_pre-0 actually is (test x*rms against it
first), then compare attn_norm-0 against the stage's POST-norm output using the same node discipline - the last
comparison mixed two different nodes and that, not the stage, produced the 20% figure.

WHAT THE DUMP'S hc_attn_pre-0 IS - narrowed, not yet named

    manifest  hc_attn_pre-0  ne=[4096,5,1,1]    (a 4096-vector per token - NOT the 16384-float stream block)
    hc_init's four streams are IDENTICAL   (mean-over-streams and stream-0 give the same 0.753 rel error,
                                           and hc_init = repeat_4d(reshape_3d(inp)) per the phase-8 notes)
    pre = [6.1547250e-04  5.9876317e-01  2.5748528e-04  6.5421796e-04]   <- stream 1 dominates
    layer_in = sum_h pre[h] * x[h] = 0.600290358 * x0
    dump node / layer_in : mean 0.9489 (std 3.3e-02, inflated by division near zeros)
    dump node / x0       : ~0.5696

so the dump's node is the SAME SHAPE as layer_in - a scaled copy of the identical stream - with a slightly
different gate scalar.  Candidates for that scalar, none tested: pre formed without the +HC_EPS and before the
sigmoid's scale[0] application, a different layer's hc weights, or the graph's own hc_attn_pre being the value
that feeds the mix rather than the value the mix produces.

NONE OF THIS IS STAGE 1's PROBLEM, and that is the point worth carrying forward: stage 1's hc_pre agrees with the
phase-6-validated oracle to seven significant digits on this exact input, and the discrepancy is entirely in my
identification of the reference node.  The stage's next real gate is the norm and then the KDA/MLA call, and it
should be judged against the oracle rather than against a dump node whose meaning has not been established.

########  STAGE 1 VERIFIED - BOTH HALVES  ########

Gated against the phase-6-validated oracle (not against a dump node), token 0 of the same hc_init:

    oracle  hc_pre layer_in : -0.016211817  0.0226506591  0.182561547
    engine  hc_pre output   : -0.0162118152 0.0226506572  0.182561572      matches to 7 significant digits

    oracle  norm(layer_in)*w: -0.359747499  0.424176991   3.25002813
    engine  attn_norm-0     : -0.359747499  0.424176991   3.25002776      rel diff 5.364e-10 of scale

So hc_pre -> rms_norm -> *attn_norm.weight is correct end to end: the gate on the mix, the eps (1e-5, the
graph's norm eps and not hyper_connection.epsilon), the plain multiply with no (1+w) folding, and the four weight
tensors (one dequantized Q8_0, three from the arena) which were independently confirmed bit-exact against the
artifact.

WHAT WENT WRONG IN THE COMPARISONS, since it cost most of this session and will cost it again:
  * the 5.6% "error" was the dump's hc_attn_pre-0, which is the same SHAPE as layer_in but a different gate
    scalar (~0.5696*x0 vs 0.6003*x0) - not layer_in at all.
  * the 20% "error" in attn_norm-0 was the same dump node's sibling, and it too disagrees with the oracle.
  * hc_init appears TWICE in the dump manifest, and the harness takes one of them; the node values a dump
    reports may therefore belong to a different segment than the input being read.
  * CONCLUSION: gate the engine against the ORACLE (glm5_hc_reference.py / glm5_kda_reference.py, both validated
    in phase 6/7), and use the dump only to find WHICH node to look at, never as the expected value.

NEXT IN 9.2, with the same discipline: the attention call (KDA on blocks with KDA, MLA on the others) against the
matching reference module, then hc_post, then the ffn stage (leading dense below block 3, moe_forward above), and
finally the head - with glm5_e2e_test.py (argmax 12089) as the terminal gate.

## 9.2 NEXT STAGE - THE ATTENTION CALL (KDA first, MLA after)

KdaWeights (include/strata/kernels/glm_kda.hpp) is 16 pointers, and they map onto bound tensor names one for one -
the binding already serves every one of them, conv_q/k/v already repacked into the [d_conv][d_inner] the kernel
indexes:

    attn_norm  <- blk.N.attn_norm.weight          (F32, and stage 1 already verifies this one)
    wq wk wv   <- blk.N.attn_q / attn_k / attn_v .weight        (Q8_0 native, dim-swapped by native serving)
    conv_q/k/v <- blk.N.ssm_conv1d_q/k/v .weight                (F32, repacked by the binding)
    ssm_a      <- blk.N.ssm_a                 (kernel wants -exp(A_log), per the field comment)
    dt_bias    <- blk.N.ssm_dt.bias
    ssm_f_a/b  <- blk.N.ssm_f_a / ssm_f_b .weight        ([hd][n_embd], [d_inner][hd])
    ssm_beta   <- blk.N.ssm_beta.weight                  ([nh][n_embd])
    ssm_g_a/b  <- blk.N.ssm_g_a / ssm_g_b .weight
    o_norm     <- blk.N.ssm_norm.weight                  ([hd], the ssm_norm gain)
    wo         <- blk.N.attn_output.weight               ([n_embd][d_inner])

The gate, and it is a direct one - the reference exposes exactly what is needed:

    glm5_kda_reference.py:117   kda_block(w, x, tokens, want_state=False)     w = load_weights(m, layer)  (:167)
    engine                      kda_forward(w, g, x, tokens, out, ...)

so: build KdaGeometry once (d_inner, d_conv, nh, hd, n_embd, eps values), fill KdaWeights from the bound tensors
by the mapping above, call kda_forward, and compare against kda_block on the same input - gating on the ORACLE,
per the rule recorded above, not on any dump node.

Two things to carry in from the measurements already made: the conv orientation is already handled (the binding
repacks to [d_conv][d_inner], so the kernel's conv_w[k*d_inner + ch] indexing is satisfied), and the swiglu clamp
of 10.0 applies to the ffn stage rather than here.  The T>1 state carry - phase 8's full debugging cycle - only
matters once more than one token is run per call, which this gate does not need.

KDA STAGE - WHERE IT STANDS, AND THE NEXT ACTION EXACTLY

The KDA gate is one step from running, and the step is a single diagnostic:

  * KdaGeometry{4096, 64, 128, 4} and kda_forward(w, g, x, tokens, out, ...) are confirmed
    (include/strata/kernels/glm_kda.hpp:81), and kda_forward applies attn_norm internally - so the gate feeds
    it the PRE-NORM hc_pre output and compares against kda_block(load_weights(m, 0), <same x>, 1), which
    normalizes internally too.  Feeding the post-norm value would double-normalize.
  * the tensor mapping (16 fields, recorded above) is right, BUT a lookup of those names in bound.tensors
    returned MISSING for all sixteen - while the print loop reads blk.0.attn_norm.weight in the same run.  So
    the names as they sit in bound.tensors are NOT simply "blk.<n>.<suffix>": they may carry a layer prefix, or
    the print loop may be printing a name taken from somewhere other than Tensor::name.

  NEXT ACTION, in order:
    1. print bound.tensors[i].name for i < 8 (one fprintf) and read the real names off the screen - do NOT
       assume the format again, which is what produced the MISSING result;
    2. fill KdaWeights from those names, call kda_forward on the pre-norm hc_pre output;
    3. copy the output back with cudaMemcpyDeviceToHost, write it to a file, and compare elementwise against
       kda_block's first return value - elementwise, not min/max/sum, since that is now cheap and the oracle
       gives the whole array.

  AND THE PROCESS RULE, learned the hard way twice today: this driver must be edited by regenerating it or by
  ONE targeted patch, never by multi-anchor string surgery - the attempt above deleted part of the stage block
  and compiled stale, which is indistinguishable from a wrong result until the error text is read.

KDA STAGE - THE NAME MYSTERY IS SOLVED, AND WHAT THE TENSOR SHAPES ALREADY SAY

1. WHY THE LOOKUP FAILED.  The print loop does not print Tensor::name - it prints the name with the prefix added
   in the printf itself:

       std::printf("blk.%d.%s\tne=%d,%d\n", block, t.name.c_str(), t.ne0, t.ne1);

   so the stored names are BARE: "attn_norm.weight", "attn_q.weight", "ssm_conv1d_q.weight".  A key built as
   "blk.0." + suffix can never match, which is exactly what the MISSING result was.  Look up by the bare name.

2. THE KDA GATE NEEDS FLOAT WEIGHTS.  attn_q/k/v are Q8_0 natively, and the branch that dequantizes at bind time
   (glm_bind.cpp:120) currently requires the name to end in "_fn.weight".  Generalising it to any quantized tensor
   requires the PLAN ROW's field name, which is not `quantized` - the row struct is GlmTensorRow and the field is
   one of the members listed above.  One read settles it; do not guess the member name again, which is what broke
   this attempt.

3. A FINDING THAT SAVES THE NEXT SESSION A CYCLE: the bound attention matrices already come out in the
   orientation the oracle uses.  The loop prints blk.0.attn_q.weight as ne=8192,4096 - i.e. [d_inner, n_embd],
   which is what glm5_kda_reference's `xn @ w["wq"].T` expects, and attn_output as ne=4096,8192 = [n_embd,
   d_inner], which is what `w["wo"]` expects.  So the KdaWeights fill is a straight name mapping with NO
   transpose, and if the gate disagrees on the first run, transposition is not the first suspect.

GENERALISING THE BIND-TIME DEQUANT - ATTEMPTED, MEASURED, REVERTED

Goal: attn_q/k/v are Q8_0 natively and the KDA kernel wants floats, so the branch at glm_bind.cpp:120 that
currently requires "_fn.weight" needed widening to any quantized tensor.  GlmTensorRow's quantized-ness is
`ttype` (glm5_blocks_gen.hpp:7 - there is no `quantized` member; that was the first compile error).

Three variants were tried and all three exit 1:

  widened to `t.ttype != 0`                        -> cudaMalloc failed for blk.0.attn_output.weight
  + `w->native_data != nullptr`                    -> same
  + exclude names containing "_exps"               -> same

so the poisoner is NOT the expert tensors (my first theory) and NOT a null pointer.  GPU memory is not exhausted
(18 MiB in use when it fails), so this is a POISONED CONTEXT: something earlier in the bind loop faults, and the
next cudaMalloc reports failure - the same signature as the host-pointer dequant fault found earlier today.
The failure lands on blk.0.attn_output.weight, which in bind order comes after attn_q/k/v, so the candidate set is
the tensors bound before it - the most likely being attn_q itself, i.e. the first tensor the widening newly
affects.  Note the branch sizes its buffer as ne0*ne1 floats and calls dequant_q8_0 with that as an ELEMENT count;
for a natively-served Q8_0 tensor the element count must come from the NATIVE tensor's own geometry, not the plan
row, and that is the first thing to verify rather than assume.

REVERTED to the last verified state: the driver binds block 0 and stage 1 prints its verified number
(-0.359747499 / 0.424176991 / 3.25002776) with STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1.  The KDA gate therefore needs either this
branch finished properly, or - much cheaper - a KDA fixture whose weights can be read as floats without it, since
attn_q/k/v are the only KDA inputs that are quantized.

THE WIDENED CONDITION BREAKS A TENSOR IT DOES NOT TOUCH - MEASURED, NOT GUESSED

Measured this time instead of theorised, and the result is the opposite of what the earlier theory predicted:

    probe (no dequant, no allocation) for blk.0.attn_q.weight:
        plan ne=4096,8192 -> n=33554432 | pack bytes=0 -> n=0 | ttype=8 | native=0x7f1e66000000

so the plan row's element count is right, ttype=8 says Q8_0, native_data is a live device pointer, and the pack
legitimately holds zero bytes for a natively-served tensor.  Every quantity the branch uses is therefore sound, and
the earlier "wrong element count" theory is DEAD.

Then, running with the condition widened to attn_q:

    STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1 STRATA_DEQUANT_ATTN_Q=1   -> cudaMalloc failed for blk.0.hc_attn_fn.weight
    STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1                           -> 0 failures

hc_attn_fn is the FIRST tensor this branch handles - nothing dequantizes before it - so a change to the CONDITION
cannot have poisoned anything upstream of it.  The failure did not move later in the bind order as a poisoning
theory requires; it appeared on a tensor the widening does not even select, and without the widening that same
tensor allocates fine (the stage runs and prints its verified number).

WHAT THAT RULES OUT: every "something earlier faults" explanation, including the two tried earlier (the experts,
a null native_data).  WHAT IT POINTS TO: the added clause itself.  The concrete next test, in isolation and with
nothing else changed, is to make the clause trivially true (e.g. widen to a name that certainly is not bound, or
replace the find() with a constant) and see whether hc_attn_fn still fails - which separates "the clause is
evaluated wrongly" from "any change to this condition shifts something else".  Note also that the condition calls
std::getenv twice and std::string::find on every row; neither is obviously dangerous, which is exactly why this
needs a clean measurement rather than a story.

REVERTED.  Verified state: 0 build errors, exit 0, stage 1 = -0.359747499 / 0.424176991 / 3.25002776.

THE WIDENED-BRANCH FAILURE - EVERY MECHANISM NOW ELIMINATED, AND THE ONE REMAINING FORK

Read rather than assumed, all in this session:

  native_dense.cpp:231-235  the upload only proceeds when the GGUF tensor's shape equals the canonical ref's
                            ne0/ne1, and sizes the buffer as native_mmvq_weight_bytes(type, ne0, ne1);
  native_mmvq.cu:1447-1452  case 8 (Q8_0) -> block_elems 32, block_bytes 34, i.e. the ordinary ggml block
                            format - so native_data IS raw Q8_0 blocks and the MMVQ-ness is in the access
                            pattern, not the storage;
  native_dense.cpp:259      native_data = item.data (a PER-TENSOR cudaMalloc), while only native_q8_1 is the
                            shared scratch - so no weight aliasing either;
  probe on blk.0.attn_q     plan ne=4096,8192 -> n=33554432 (correct), ttype=8, native_data live device
                            pointer, pack bytes=0 (legitimate: the pack holds nothing for a native tensor);
  10 consecutive runs       with STRATA_HC_DEQUANT=1 alone: 10/10 clean, 0 cudaMalloc failures, so the
                            working configuration is deterministic, not flaky.

So the element count, the buffer size, the buffer kind, the buffer's origin and the run-to-run stability are all
now cleared, and my dequant's read extents match the allocation exactly.  THE ONLY REMAINING EXPLANATION IS ORDER
OR THE CLAUSE ITSELF, and the single test that separates them is one fprintf: print every tensor the branch
enters, in the order it enters, so the actual bind order is read off the screen rather than inferred from the print
loop (which runs AFTER the bind and is therefore not the bind order - that inference is what made "hc_attn_fn is
first" look like a fact).  If attn_q dequantizes BEFORE hc_attn_fn, the upstream-poisoning explanation is alive
again and the target of interest is what attn_q's dequant does to the context; if it does not, the added clause is
implicated and should be tested trivially-true in isolation.

Note the pattern to break: four successive mechanisms were invented and then killed by one cheap read each.  The
next session should make its FIRST act the order printout, not a hypothesis.

########  THE FORK IS RESOLVED: attn_q BINDS BEFORE hc_attn_fn  ########

Read the bind order instead of inferring it, and it settles the question that occupied the last stretch.  The bind
iterates the pack's rows, so the pack index IS the bind order (index.txt, block 0):

    line  9   blk.0.attn_q.weight       8  (Q8_0)  4096 x 8192
    line 16   blk.0.hc_attn_fn.weight   8  (Q8_0)  16384 x 24

attn_q is bound SEVEN ROWS BEFORE hc_attn_fn.  With the condition widened to include attn_q, its dequant therefore
runs first, and hc_attn_fn's cudaMalloc failing afterwards is ordinary upstream poisoning - exactly the mechanism
that was ruled out on the false premise that hc_attn_fn is the first tensor the branch handles.

Where that false premise came from, and the lesson: the print loop runs AFTER the bind completes, so its order is
the canonical table's order, not the bind order.  Three conclusions in this session died to that same shape of
error - believing an ordering that was never measured.  The bind order is readable in one grep, and it should have
been the first act, not the last.

WHAT THIS MEANS FOR THE WIDENING, concretely: attn_q's dequant is the poisoner, and its extents look correct
(n=33554432 elements -> 1048576 Q8_0 blocks -> 35.6 MB read, 134 MB written into a buffer sized n*4), while
native_mmvq_weight_bytes gives exactly 34 bytes per 32 elements for case 8.  So the overrun is NOT in the extents,
and the next measurement is to find which STEP of that dequant faults rather than to reason about it further:
check cudaGetLastError() immediately after cudaMalloc, after the cudaStreamSynchronize, and after dequant_q8_0
itself, for attn_q alone - three reads of the error state, no theory.

THE FAULT IS IN attn_q's OWN DEQUANT - NAMED, NOT INFERRED

Instrumented the branch and reproduced, one run:

    dequant: blk.0.attn_q.weight   n=33554432   want=128.0 MB   alloc+dequant: an illegal memory access was encountered

So the very first tensor the widening touches faults inside its own dequant, which fully accounts for the downstream
cudaMalloc failure on hc_attn_fn (bound seven rows later) - and the order reading predicted exactly this.

The chain of elimination now stands as: element count correct (33554432) - buffer allocation correct (128 MiB) -
native buffer format correct (Q8_0, 34 bytes/32 elements) - native buffer per-tensor, not shared - run-to-run
deterministic (10/10) - bind order read from the pack index (attn_q at row 9, hc_attn_fn at row 16).  And the
kernel itself is clean: dequant_q8_0_kernel indexes with long long, dequant_q8_0 launches grid=8192, threads=128,
the last read is blocks[1048575*34 .. +34] = 35.6 MB and the last write is x[n-1], both provably inside their
buffers.

Two suspects remain, and both are cheap to settle next:
  1. the native blocks buffer for attn_q is smaller than n/32*34 = 35.6 MB, i.e. the upload sized it from a
     different pair of dims than the plan row gives - the check at native_dense.cpp:231 compares tensor.shape to
     ref.ne0/ne1, and GLM's attn_q is exactly the tensor whose GGUF shape and pack shape are TRANSPOSED of each
     other (trap 7), so this is the first place to look;
  2. the error is STICKY.  cudaGetLastError() returns the last error set by ANY earlier call in the process, and
     my report sits after cudaStreamSynchronize - so if the native upload or the 128 MiB cudaMalloc had already
     failed silently, this line would report it here and blame the dequant.  Clearing the error state right
     before the dequant (cudaGetLastError() once and discard) distinguishes the two in one run.

WHICH TO RUN FIRST: (2), because it is one line and it decides whether the fault is in the dequant at all.

STICKY ERROR RULED OUT - THE FAULT IS THE DEQUANT ITSELF, AND ONE SUSPECT REMAINS

Clearing cudaGetLastError() immediately before the call, then reporting after it:

    dequant: blk.0.attn_q.weight   n=33554432   want=128.0 MB   alloc+dequant: an illegal memory access was encountered

unchanged.  So the error is genuinely produced by this dequant, not inherited from an earlier failed upload or
allocation.  Every other link in the chain has been checked and holds: n is the right element count, the output
buffer is the right size, the kernel indexes with long long and launches on grid=8192 so neither its read
(1,048,576 blocks x 34 bytes = 35.6 MB) nor its write (up to x[n-1]) can leave its own buffers, and the run is
deterministic.

WHAT IS LEFT: the blocks buffer that native_data points at for attn_q is smaller than the 35.6 MB this dequant
reads from it.  The reason to look there specifically, and not anywhere else: attn_q is exactly the tensor whose
GGUF shape and pack shape are TRANSPOSED of one another (trap 7 - the pack's row says 4096x8192, the artifact's
tensor is 8192x4096), and the loader's upload sizes that buffer from whichever pair of dims it settled on in
native_dense.cpp:231.  The element count is identical either way, so nothing upstream notices; only a reader that
walks the blocks by a count derived from the OTHER orientation would run off the end - and the hc function
matrices, which work, are square-ish in exactly the sense that hides the difference (16384x24 and 24 either way).

NEXT ACT, one line: in native_dense.cpp, print the bytes it allocates for a named tensor together with
tensor.shape[0]/shape[1] and ref.ne0/ref.ne1, and run for blk.0.attn_q.weight.  That either shows a buffer smaller
than 35.6 MB, which is the answer, or shows it is the full size - in which case the dequant's own arguments are
wrong in a way the extents cannot reveal and the reader's stride must be checked against the recorded block layout.

########  ROOT CAUSE FOUND AND FIXED: THE DEQUANT ASSUMED A BLOCK SIZE  ########

The last link, printed rather than argued - what native_dense.cpp allocates versus what the reader asks for:

    native upload blk.0.attn_q.weight: gguf shape=4096,8192  ref=4096,8192
        alloc = 23,068,672 B        (= 22 bytes per 32 elements)
        read  = 35,651,584 B        (= 34 bytes per 32 elements, Q8_0)

The buffer is 12.6 MB smaller than what the dequant reads, so the reader walked off the end - the illegal memory
access, and through it the poisoned context that made a later cudaMalloc fail on a tensor bound seven rows later.
The ratio names the cause exactly: 23068672 / 33554432 * 32 = 22, and native_mmvq_weight_bytes gives 22 bytes per
32 elements only for CASE 6.  blk.0.attn_q.weight is not Q8_0 in this artifact; hc_attn_fn is, which is why one
worked and the other faulted.  blk.3.attn_q_b.weight shows alloc == read to the byte, confirming the type varies
per tensor rather than the formula being wrong globally.

THE FIX: gate the branch on the type the dequantizer actually implements.

    } else if (std::getenv("STRATA_HC_DEQUANT") && w->native_type == 8 && ...

Verified both ways: with the widening the run now finishes clean (exit 0, 0 failures, both _fn tensors
"no error"), and the default path still prints stage 1 = -0.359747499 / 0.424176991 / 3.25002776.

THE GENERAL RULE, and it is the one worth keeping: a dequantizer must be SELECTED BY THE TENSOR'S ACTUAL TYPE,
never assumed from what the tensor is called or from what the pack's row claims.  And when a buffer-size formula
and a reader disagree, print BOTH numbers - alloc versus read - because the ratio between them names the cause
immediately (22 vs 34 bytes per block here), while every argument about extents, layouts, ordering and pointer
kinds cannot.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE KDA GATE: attn_q/k/v stay natively quantized for this artifact (types other than 8), so the
gate still needs either a dequantizer for the type they use or F32 attention weights in its fixture.  What is
gone is the hazard: widening the branch can no longer poison the context.

HOW THE KDA GATE GETS FLOAT WEIGHTS - THE OPTIONS, MEASURED AS FAR AS THEY GO

The engine's dequantizers are exactly: dequant_q8_0 and dequant_q8_K (quantize_act.hpp), plus dequant_f32 /
dequant_f16 / dequant_bf16 and dequant_s2 for other paths.  There is NO dequantizer for the 22-byte-block type
that blk.0.attn_q.weight actually uses (native_mmvq_weight_bytes case 6), which is why the widened branch read past
the end before it was gated by native_type == 8.

And the pack does not hand attn_q/k/v over as floats either: a default-configuration run reports 12 rows still
"=quantized" in block 0, attn_q/k/v among them, so native serving is the only source for those three.

Three routes to a KDA gate, in order of what they cost:

  1. WRITE THE MISSING DEQUANTIZER.  A Q5_1-style dequantizer for the 22-byte type, with its own parity gate
     against ggml's own conversion (the same discipline the q8_0 one had).  Extensible, and it is the real
     engineering answer - but it is a new kernel plus a new gate, not a step.
  2. GATE THE KERNEL WITH FIXTURE WEIGHTS.  Have the Python side write the oracle's own wq/wk/wv to a file and let
     the driver load them as floats.  Works today and validates kda_forward end to end.  Its limit is the standing
     rule: a gate that builds its own fixtures validates the kernel and never the wiring, so it must be followed by
     a real binding check once route 1 exists.
  3. GATE ONLY WHAT IS ALREADY FLOAT.  Everything else the KDA call consumes in block 0 IS float in the pack -
     conv_q/k/v, ssm_a, ssm_dt.bias, ssm_f_a/b, ssm_beta, ssm_g_a/b, ssm_norm, attn_output and attn_norm are all
     F32 rows - so the smallest real gate is to run kda_forward with wq/wk/wv supplied from a fixture and every
     other weight taken from the engine's own binding.

Route 3 is the recommended next step: it exercises the engine's binding for eleven of the sixteen KDA inputs, needs
one small driver addition (read three matrices from a file), and keeps the fixture honest about exactly which
inputs it substitutes.

STATE: default path exit 0, stage 1 = -0.359747499 / 0.424176991 / 3.25002776.  The widened path exits 0 with 0
failures now that the type guard is in place.

KDA GATE FIXTURE, EXPECTED SIDE - READY

Built from the phase-6-validated oracle on the SAME input stage 1 consumes (token 0 of the reference's hc_init,
carried through the oracle's hc_pre), and saved to /home/peb/moredata/glm5-kda-l0-t1/:

    input.bin   (1, 4096)      the pre-norm hc_pre output, what kda_forward takes
    result.bin  (1, 4096)      min -0.00368443666  max 0.00369193095  sum -0.120586747

plus every intermediate the oracle exposes, which is what makes this a USABLE gate rather than a pass/fail one -
each is a staged comparison point matching the phase-8 failure list:

    xn    (1, 4096)       min -0.359747    max  0.424177     <- identical to stage 1's verified output
    qc    (1, 8192)       min -0.0865774   max  0.0200543    conv q, pre-L2
    kc    (1, 8192)       min -0.0104094   max  0.0993052
    vc    (1, 8192)       min -0.0139548   max  0.0347515
    g     (1, 64, 128)    min -4.383       max -4.701e-05    the gate, before exp
    beta  (1, 64)         min  0.41576     max  0.625428
    q     (1, 64, 128)    min -0.958223    max  0.783057     after l2_norm
    k     (1, 64, 128)    min -0.631265    max  0.997239
    v     (1, 64, 128)    min -0.0139548   max  0.0347515
    attn  (1, 64, 128)    min -0.000196751 max  0.000282776  the recurrence output
    o     (1, 8192)       min -0.00430178  max  0.00552851
    (one intermediate is None - a state-related key the oracle does not populate for want_state=False)

xn matching stage 1's number exactly is the useful cross-check: the KDA gate's input is stage 1's verified output,
so a failure at the first comparison point (qc) would be a conv/weight issue, not a chain-wiring one.

WHAT REMAINS: the driver side - read wq/wk/wv from a fixture file as floats, take the other eleven KDA weights from
the engine's own binding, call kda_forward on input.bin, and compare stage by stage against these files. F32
attention weights are needed only because the engine has no dequantizer for the 22-byte type those three use.

########  KDA GATE PASSES - 8/8 STAGES ON THE ARTIFACT'S REAL WEIGHTS  ########

tools/kda_gate.cpp (new target kda_gate) drives kda_forward with the fixture's own weights, input and expected
values, and compares every stage the kernel exposes against the oracle's file for it:

    result   rel 3.532e-06  PASS        q      rel 2.037e-06  PASS
    g        rel 3.808e-07  PASS        k      rel 1.464e-06  PASS
    beta     rel 2.859e-07  PASS        v      rel 8.844e-07  PASS
                                        attn   rel 1.750e-06  PASS
                                        o      rel 1.895e-06  PASS
    KDA GATE: PASS

Three things this establishes, and three it does NOT:

  ESTABLISHED
  * kda_forward is correct on block 0's REAL weights - not a synthetic fixture - right through the recurrence
    (attn at 1.75e-06), the L2-normalised q/k, the gate g, beta, and the final o.  Phase 7 gated this kernel on
    hand-made inputs; this gates it on the artifact.
  * kda_forward is HOST code: src/kernels/glm_kda.cpp takes plain std::vector::data() pointers, exactly like
    hc_pre, so no CUDA plumbing, streams or device copies are involved.  That is worth knowing before anyone
    wraps it in device buffers.
  * the fixture's own consistency: its input is stage 1's verified output, so the two gates now chain.

  NOT ESTABLISHED - state this wherever the result is reported
  * T > 1.  This gate runs ONE token.  Phase 8 measured that the multi-token recurrence FAILS (t=3/t=5: attn
    3.71e-01 of scale, while t=1 passes ~1e-06), and nothing here exercises that path.  A single-token gate must
    not be read as full KDA verification.
  * the BINDING of these sixteen tensors.  The weights come from the oracle's own files, so this validates the
    kernel and never the wiring - the rule recorded earlier in this file.  What the engine's binding produces for
    attn_q/k/v (natively quantized, 22-byte blocks) is still unchecked, and cannot be checked as floats until
    either a dequantizer for that type exists or the KDA weights are read from the pack.
  * ssm_a's convention: the fixture's values are all NEGATIVE (-8.159 .. -1.417), i.e. already -exp(A_log) as the
    KdaWeights comment requires, so the kernel and the oracle agree on that reading - but it is agreement, not an
    independent check of the convention against the metadata.

NEXT: the T>1 case is the honest next gate - reuse this harness with tokens=2,3,5, dumping the oracle's intermediates
for each token count, since the recurrence is the one part of KDA that is known to have failed before.

########  KDA GATE PASSES AT T=1 AND T=5 - THE MULTI-TOKEN RECURRENCE IS CLEAN  ########

Same gate, same weights, two token counts, both fixtures built by the phase-6-validated oracle:

    T=1:  result 3.53e-06  g 3.81e-07  beta 2.86e-07  q 2.04e-06  k 1.46e-06  v 8.84e-07  attn 1.75e-06  o 1.90e-06
    T=5:  result 3.57e-06  g 5.80e-07  beta 3.71e-07  q 1.84e-06  k 2.03e-06  v 1.34e-06  attn 2.94e-06  o 3.03e-06
    KDA GATE: PASS (both)

attn at T=5 is the number that matters: phase 8 measured that same stage at 3.71e-01 of scale and recorded it as a
multi-token recurrence bug.  It is now 2.94e-06.

WHAT THAT DOES AND DOES NOT MEAN - stated carefully, because the tempting reading is wrong:

  * The honest reading is that the kernel's recurrence was never the culprit at T>1.  This gate feeds kda_forward
    the oracle's own weights through plain host pointers; the phase-8 failure was observed through the port's
    chain, where the conv tensors and the weight arena were both suspect and both have since changed.  The
    recurrence itself now agrees with the reference to 3e-06 with nothing else in the loop, so the earlier failure
    is best explained as wiring rather than arithmetic.
  * It is NOT evidence that the T>1 defect was "fixed" - no specific fix was made to the recurrence, and claiming
    one would be inventing a history.  What is true is that the stage is now measured clean under controlled
    inputs.
  * It does NOT cover the binding.  Every weight here comes from a file written by the oracle, so this is the
    kernel and never the wiring - the standing rule.  The engine's own binding of these sixteen tensors is still
    unverified, and attn_q/k/v in particular cannot be bound as floats until a dequantizer exists for the 22-byte
    type they use.
