STATE: stage 1 verified; KDA gate PASS at T=1 and T=5; glm5_bind_check exit 0 on the default path; the widened
dequant path exits 0 with the native_type == 8 guard in place.  Fixtures: glm5-kda-l0-t1 and glm5-kda-l0-t5, each

same block.

BINDING CHECK FOR THE F32 KDA TENSORS - WHAT IS VERIFIED, AND A BROKEN COMPARATOR

Verified by READING THE RAW DRIVER LINES against the oracle's own values, which is the only method that has not
misled this session:

    blk.0.attn_norm.weight      engine  +0.0703125  +0.157226562  420.698242
                                oracle  +0.0703125  +0.15722656   420.698242        EXACT
    blk.0.ssm_conv1d_q.weight   engine  -0.3984375  +0.2109375   -0.70519589
                                oracle  -0.3984375  +0.2109375                     EXACT

Those two are the two structural classes that matter: a plain F32 vector straight from the arena, and one of the
CONV tensors, which the binding repacks into [d_conv][d_inner] and therefore could easily have arrived permuted or
transposed.  Both match the oracle exactly, so the repack is right at least in extent and scale, and the F32 path
is right for this tensor.

Honest limits: this is 2 of 13 by eye, min/max/sum only, and sum matching does not prove the conv tensor's internal
ordering - it excludes a wrong tensor, a wrong scale and a wrong element count, not a permutation.

THE COMPARATOR IS BROKEN, and it is worth recording because it kept looking like engine failures:
tools/kda_binding_check.py parses 0 rows from the driver's output.  The driver separates fields with the two
characters backslash + t (a literal '\\t' in its printf - confirmed by repr()), and I failed to express that
correctly four times in a row: twice in shell one-liners where quoting ate a backslash (producing absurd values
read out of the hash field, which looked like 13 real failures), and twice in the script.  Every one of those
failures was my measurement, not the engine.  A binding verdict must not be reported from this script until it
parses the row count it expects - the script now refuses to report with 0 rows parsed, which is the behaviour to
keep.

WHAT THE NEXT SESSION SHOULD DO FIRST: fix that regex against a repr() of the actual line (not against what the
format is believed to be), confirm it parses 26 rows for block 0, and only then read its verdicts.  The engine-side
facts it should then check are the remaining eleven F32 tensors plus, separately, whether wq/wk/wv can be verified
at all without a dequantizer for their 22-byte type.

THE MISTAKE, recorded because it is now the most expensive single one of the session (seven attempts):
repr() renders a real TAB as '\t', and od -c renders a real tab as '\t' as well.  I read both as the two-character
sequence backslash-t, concluded the driver emitted a literal '\t', and wrote seven parsers to match that
misreading.  A literal backslash-t would show as '\\t' in repr and as TWO separate columns in od -c.  The one
command that would have ended it immediately - printing repr(line), len(line.split('\t')) and the parts list for a
single known-good line - was written into this file as the next action and then skipped seven times in favour of
editing the parser and re-running.  Both instruments were right; my reading of them was wrong.

WHAT IS NOW COVERED: stage 1 (hc_pre 7 significant digits, norm 5.4e-10), the KDA kernel at T=1 and T=5 (8/8 stages

(native_type == 8 guard).

    34 B/32 = Q8_0 (type 8)          hc_attn_fn  hc_ffn_fn  ssm_beta  ssm_f_a  ssm_f_b  ssm_g_a  ssm_g_b

    26 B/32 = a third type           ffn_down

So five of the nine "quantized" KDA tensors were already Q8_0, i.e. already handled by dequant_q8_0 - they were
excluded only by the branch's NAME filter.  Keying the branch on the TYPE alone (native_type == 8, opt-in via
STRATA_DEQUANT_ALL_Q8_0) is safe BY CONSTRUCTION: type 8 is exactly what that dequantizer implements, which is the
same guard that fixed the root cause.  Result, with the checker finally parsing:

    attn_norm 3.2e-09  conv_q 1.0e-11  conv_k 1.1e-11  conv_v 1.6e-09  ssm_a 1.3e-08  dt_bias 4.4e-09

    -> 12 PASS, 0 FAIL, 1 quantized (wo)      every dequant reported "no error"

THE ONLY REMAINING GAP IS ONE TYPE.  wq, wk, wv and wo are all the 22-byte type.  A single dequantizer for it -
with its own parity gate against ggml's conversion, as the Q8_0 one has - closes the binding completely AND lets
kda_gate run on the engine's own bound weights instead of the oracle's fixture files, which is the same piece of
work.  That is the next step, and it is now narrowly specified: one type, four tensors, two gates.

THE REMAINING TYPE, MEASURED EXACTLY - AND A CORRECTION OF MY OWN READING

Derived from the trace's alloc-bytes and element counts, testing every block geometry in the engine's own table
for an EXACT fit rather than dividing and assuming 32-element blocks:

    attn_q  attn_k  attn_v  attn_output  ffn_gate  ffn_up   ->  256 elems/block x 176 B   (exact)
    ffn_down                                                ->  256 elems/block x 210 B   (exact)
    ssm_beta (and the other Q8_0 ones)                      ->   32 elems/block x  34 B   (exact)

So the "22 bytes per 32 elements" I reported earlier was an artifact of my own division - 176/8 happens to be 22 -
and the actual block is FOUR TIMES larger.  Cross-referencing native_mmvq.cu's table (case 13: 256/176,
case 14: 256/210) and the standard ggml sizes (block_q5_K = 2+2+12+32+128 = 176 bytes, block_q6_K = 210 bytes),
these are K-quants: 176 B/256 = the type at case 13, 210 B/256 = case 14.  Verify against ggml-common.h rather than
against these names - but the geometry is now measured, not inferred.

WHAT THAT MEANS FOR THE WORK, stated plainly because it is bigger than it looked:

  * attn_q, attn_k, attn_v and attn_output are K-quants with 6-bit packed scales and separate high-bit planes.
    A dequantizer for them is real work - dequantize_row_q5_K transcribed from llama.cpp the way nearest_int and
    the Q8_0 path already were ("transcribed rather than replaced") - plus its own parity gate against ggml's own
    conversion, not a few lines like the Q8_0 case.
  * ffn_down needs the 210-byte type (Q6_K) IF the ffn stage is to run on bound weights; the KDA gate does not need
    it, so it can wait.
  * THE PAYOFF IS STILL TWO GATES FOR ONE KERNEL: the K-quant dequantizer closes the binding check (wq/wk/wv/wo
    become comparable) and lets kda_gate run on the engine's own bound weights instead of the oracle's fixture
    files.  The KDA work itself is finished; this is what stands between it and being wired end to end.

on both paths.  Fixtures glm5-kda-l0-t1 and glm5-kda-l0-t5, gate tools/kda_gate.cpp, checker
tools/kda_binding_check.py - all committed and working.

THE REMAINING TYPES VERIFIED AGAINST GGML ITSELF - NOT AGAINST NAMES OR MY ARITHMETIC

    395-404  ggml.h:  GGML_TYPE_Q5_0 = 6    GGML_TYPE_Q5_K = 13    GGML_TYPE_Q6_K = 14

                            uint8_t qs[128]; }  -> 2+2+12+32+128 = 176 bytes
    362-367  ggml-common.h:  block_q6_K { uint8_t ql[128]; uint8_t qh[64]; int8_t scales[16]; ggml_half d; }
                            -> 128+64+16+2 = 210 bytes
    both carry a static_assert(sizeof(...) == ...) so the sizes are compiler-checked, not documentation

176 and 210 are exactly the two geometries measured from the trace's allocation sizes, and the type NUMBERS match
the engine's own switch in native_mmvq.cu (case 13: 256 elems / 176 B, case 14: 256 / 210).  So the identification
is now confirmed from two independent directions - the artifact's byte counts and ggml's own struct sizes - and the
earlier chain of, first, calling this "22 bytes per 32 elements" and, second, calling the 26-byte reading a "third
type", are both retired: 22 was 176/8 and 26 was 210*32/256.  One type family, K-quants, and two block sizes.

THE WORK, FULLY SPECIFIED FOR WHOEVER PICKS IT UP:

     "transcribed, not replaced" style as nearest_int and the Q8_0 path, exposing the same
     `void dequant_qX_K(const uint8_t* blocks, float* x, int64_t n, void* stream)` shape;

  3. only then widen the bind-time dequant from native_type == 8 to {8, 13}, which is safe for the same reason 8
     alone was: the guard must match the types a dequantizer actually implements;

################################################################
## TRANSCRIPTION SOURCE FOR STEP 1 - the reference to copy, verbatim from
## /home/peb/llama.cpp-glm5/ggml/src/ggml-quants.c
## (QK_K = 256, K_SCALE_SIZE = 12; the engine already transcribes nearest_int and the Q8_0 path the same way)
################################################################

--- static inline void get_scale_min_k4(int j, const uint8_t * q, uint8_t * d, uint8_t * m)
static inline void get_scale_min_k4(int j, const uint8_t * GGML_RESTRICT q, uint8_t * GGML_RESTRICT d, uint8_t * GGML_RESTRICT m) {
    if (j < 4) {
        *d = q[j] & 63; *m = q[j + 4] & 63;
    } else {
        *d = (q[j+4] & 0xF) | ((q[j-4] >> 6) << 4);
        *m = (q[j+4] >>  4) | ((q[j-0] >> 6) << 4);
    }
}

//========================- 2-bit (de)-quantization

void quantize_row_q2_K_ref(const float * GGML_RESTRICT x, block_q2_K * GGML_RESTRICT y, int64_t k) {
    assert(k % QK_K == 0);
    const int nb = k / QK_K;


--- void dequantize_row_q5_K(const block_q5_K * restrict x, float * restrict y, int64_t k)
void dequantize_row_q5_K(const block_q5_K * GGML_RESTRICT x, float * GGML_RESTRICT y, int64_t k) {
    assert(k % QK_K == 0);
    const int64_t nb = k / QK_K;

    for (int i = 0; i < nb; i++) {
        const uint8_t * ql = x[i].qs;
        const uint8_t * qh = x[i].qh;

        const float d = GGML_FP16_TO_FP32(x[i].d);
        const float min = GGML_FP16_TO_FP32(x[i].dmin);

        int is = 0;
        uint8_t sc, m;
        uint8_t u1 = 1, u2 = 2;
        for (int j = 0; j < QK_K; j += 64) {
            get_scale_min_k4(is + 0, x[i].scales, &sc, &m);
            const float d1 = d * sc; const float m1 = min * m;
            get_scale_min_k4(is + 1, x[i].scales, &sc, &m);
            const float d2 = d * sc; const float m2 = min * m;
            for (int l = 0; l < 32; ++l) *y++ = d1 * ((ql[l] & 0xF) + (qh[l] & u1 ? 16 : 0)) - m1;
            for (int l = 0; l < 32; ++l) *y++ = d2 * ((ql[l]  >> 4) + (qh[l] & u2 ? 16 : 0)) - m2;
            ql += 32; is += 2;
            u1 <<= 2; u2 <<= 2;
        }
    }
}

static void quantize_row_q5_K_impl(const float * GGML_RESTRICT x, block_q5_K * GGML_RESTRICT y, int64_t n_per_row, const float * quant_weights) {
    assert(n_per_row % QK_K == 0);
    const int64_t nb = n_per_row / QK_K;

as { half d; half dmin; u8 scales[12]; u8 qh[32]; u8 qs[128] } and writing 256 floats per block (QK_K = 256, the

STEP 2 FIXTURE READY: /home/peb/moredata/glm5-q5k-parity

    raw_q5k_blocks.bin        23,068,672 bytes = 131,072 blocks of 176 B, taken from GGUF shard 2
    oracle_dequantised.bin    33,554,432 floats, the oracle's own values for the same tensor

TWO INDEPENDENT CONFIRMATIONS CAME OUT OF BUILDING THIS, neither of which was the point of building it:

  * 131,072 blocks x 256 elements = 33,554,432 elements exactly, and the raw byte count is 23,068,672 - IDENTICAL to
    the alloc size the engine's own trace reported for blk.0.attn_q.weight.  So the engine's native buffer really is
    this tensor's raw Q5_K blocks, which is a second, independent confirmation of the type identification (the
    first was that 176 and 210 match ggml's compiler-checked struct sizes).
  * the oracle's min/max for blk.0.attn_q.weight are -0.374668 / +0.404713, which are exactly the values in the
    KDA fixture's w_wq.bin - so blk.0.attn_q.weight IS the oracle's wq, as the weight mapping assumed.  The mapping
    recorded earlier in this file is confirmed against the data rather than by name.

Also worth keeping: the tensor lives in GGUF SHARD 2, not shard 1.  The driver is given shard 1 for metadata and
scans siblings; anything reading a specific tensor must scan the shards rather than assume.

WHAT REMAINS FOR STEP 2 - the tool, in full: declare `void dequant_q5_K(const uint8_t*, float*, int64_t, void*)`
in include/strata/kernels/quantize_act.hpp (next to dequant_q8_0's declaration), then a small host tool that reads
raw_q5k_blocks.bin, cudaMallocs the output, calls dequant_q5_K, copies back and writes floats; compare elementwise
against oracle_dequantised.bin in python.  About forty lines and one CMake target, but it needs the header touched,
a new file and a build - four steps, and this file should be the sixth port artifact that gets built in one pass
rather than a fifth that gets patched repeatedly.

########  STEP 2 DONE: dequant_q5_K IS BIT-EXACT AGAINST THE ORACLE  ########

    raw blocks   131,072 x 176 B = 23,068,672 B, from GGUF shard 2, blk.0.attn_q.weight
    engine       min -0.374668121  max +0.404712915
    oracle       min -0.374668     max +0.404713
    ELEMENTWISE  worst absolute difference 0.000e+00 over 33,554,432 elements
                 elements differing by more than 1e-5 of scale: 0
    Q5_K PARITY GATE: PASS

Not within a tolerance - EXACTLY equal, element for element, over a 134 MB tensor.  The transcribed
get_scale_min_k4 and dequantize_row_q5_K are therefore right, and right for the reasons that matter rather than by
coincidence: the 6-bit scale/min unpacking, the two high-bit planes (u1/u2 shifted by 2 per 64-element group), the
low/high nibble split within ql, and the per-block d/dmin halves all agree with an implementation that was written
from the ggml spec independently of the C this was copied from.

TOOLS: tools/q5k_parity.cpp (reads the raw blocks, runs the kernel, writes engine_output.bin, reports stats) with
its own CMake target q5k_parity; the comparison itself is np.fromfile on the two files.  Fixture:
/home/peb/moredata/glm5-q5k-parity.  This is the fourth working gate artifact in the port alongside glm5_bind_check,
kda_gate and kda_binding_check.py.

NEXT, and it is now small: widen the bind-time dequant's guard from native_type == 8 to {8, 13} and dispatch to the
kernel by type - the branch currently calls dequant_q8_0 unconditionally, so a type switch is needed, not just a
condition change.  That is safe for the same reason the type guard was: each dequantizer is called only for the
type it implements, and this one has now been proven bit-exact rather than merely compiled.  Then
tools/kda_binding_check.py should report 13 PASS and zero quantized, and kda_gate can be re-run with the engine's
own bound weights instead of the oracle's fixture files - the last gate before the chain continues past attention.

########  STEP 3 DONE: THE KDA BINDING IS COMPLETE - 13 PASS, 0 FAIL, 0 QUANTIZED  ########

Guard widened to {8, 13} with a dispatch on type (and a hard error for any type with no dequantizer, so an
unhandled type fails loudly instead of being read as the wrong block size - which is precisely the bug that cost
this session its first half):

    attn_norm 3.2e-09   ssm_f_a  8.6e-10   o_norm 3.2e-10
    conv_q    1.0e-11   ssm_f_b  8.2e-10   wo     8.5e-10   <- via Q5_K, was "quantized" before
    conv_k    1.1e-11   ssm_beta 3.0e-10
    conv_v    1.6e-09   ssm_g_a  1.0e-09
    ssm_a     1.3e-08   ssm_g_b  3.9e-11
    dt_bias   4.4e-09
    -> 13 PASS, 0 FAIL, 0 quantized        run exit 0, dequant errors 0

wq/wk/wv are not among those thirteen because the checker's table never listed them - they were the ones that could
not be compared, so they were left out and not put back.  They ARE now covered, by a stronger check than min/max:
the parity run dequantized blk.0.attn_q.weight through the newly bound path and the engine's values matched the
oracle EXACTLY, element for element, over all 33,554,432 of them.  Since attn_q IS wq (confirmed earlier by
min/max against the w_wq fixture), wq is verified exactly, and wk/wv flowed through the identical code path with the
same kernel.  Adding the three keys to the checker's table would make it report sixteen in one command, and that is
worth doing before the table is trusted as a complete inventory rather than an inventory of what used to be
checkable.

STEP 4, the last one: run kda_gate on the ENGINE'S OWN bound weights instead of the oracle's fixture files.  The
fixture files exist because the weights could not be bound as floats; that reason is now gone.  That is the gate
which proves the wiring end to end rather than kernel and binding separately, and after it the chain can continue
past the attention call.

########  THE KDA BINDING IS FULLY VERIFIED: 16 PASS, 0 FAIL, 0 QUANTIZED  ########

Every weight kda_forward consumes, bound by the engine's own loader and compared against the oracle:

    attn_norm 3.2e-09   ssm_a    1.3e-08   ssm_beta 3.0e-10   wo      8.5e-10
    wq        1.0e-09   dt_bias  4.4e-09   ssm_g_a  1.0e-09
    wk        4.2e-10   ssm_f_a  8.6e-10   ssm_g_b  3.9e-11
    wv        1.0e-09   ssm_f_b  8.2e-10   o_norm   3.2e-10
    conv_q    1.0e-11   conv_k   1.1e-11   conv_v   1.6e-09
    -> 16 PASS, 0 FAIL, 0 quantized

wq, wk and wv are the three that the whole K-quant effort existed for, and they land at 1.0e-09, 4.2e-10 and
1.0e-09.  The checker's table also now lists them, so this is one command rather than a set of partial reports -
which matters, because a table that silently omits the hard cases reports success without covering them, and this
one did exactly that an hour ago.

WHAT THE BINDING WORK ITEM TOOK, end to end, all of it recorded above: identifying that attn_q/k/v are Q5_K rather
than Q8_0 (via alloc-vs-read, after two wrong readings of my own); confirming it against ggml's compiler-checked
struct sizes; transcribing get_scale_min_k4 and dequantize_row_q5_K; gating the transcription BIT-EXACT against
the oracle over 33.5 million elements rather than against the C it came from; widening the bind-time guard to {8,
13} with a hard error for anything else; and extending the checker's inventory.  Six artifacts came out of it -
glm5_bind_check, kda_gate, kda_binding_check.py, q5k_parity, its CMake target, and two fixtures - and every one of
them still passes.

STEP 4 IS THE ONLY ONE LEFT: run kda_gate on these bound weights instead of the oracle's fixture files.  The
fixtures existed because the weights could not be bound as floats, and that reason is gone.  That gate proves the
wiring rather than kernel and binding separately, and after it the chain continues past the attention call.

STEP 4, SPECIFIED SO ITS FIRST MOVE IS MECHANICAL RATHER THAN AN INVESTIGATION

Goal: kda_gate must consume the ENGINE's bound weights instead of the oracle's fixture files, which is the gate that
proves the wiring rather than kernel and binding separately.

Where the edit goes and why there: glm5_bind_check is the only thing in this port that binds a block, so the export
has to come from it.  Add a self-contained block guarded by argc - exactly the pattern the stage-1 block uses - so
the existing path cannot be affected by it:

    if (argc > 5) {                                  // argv[5] = a directory to write the bound weights into
        for (const auto& t : bound.tensors) {        // NOTE: t.name is BARE - "attn_q.weight", no blk.N. prefix,
            ...                                      // the prefix is added by the printf, not stored (cost me a miss)
            cudaMemcpy(host.data(), t.ptr, n * sizeof(float), cudaMemcpyDeviceToHost);
            write host to <dir>/w_<name>.bin
        }
    }

The three traps, all of which have already bitten once in this session and are therefore not hypothetical:
  * the names are BARE (the earlier MISSING lookup failed because the prefix was assumed to be stored);
  * the copies must be sized by tensor KIND (quantized tensors are bytes, not floats - the bug that made the first
    copy after the loop fail);
  * the block must sit ABOVE any cudaFree(arena) in the driver - placed after it, every arena pointer reads as a
    freed allocation and reports cudaMemoryTypeUnregistered, which is what cost the first half of this session.

Then kda_gate needs no change at all if the files are named w_<oracle key>.bin as the fixture's are - or, better,
give it the directory and let it read whatever names the export produces, since matching the oracle's naming is a
convenience rather than a requirement.  Compare its result against the same fixtures: the values are expected to be
IDENTICAL, because the binding was just verified 16/16 against those very numbers.  That identity is the point - if
the bound weights and the fixture agree, then the KDA gate passing on bound weights adds exactly one thing the
current pair of gates does not: proof that the two halves compose.

STATE AT HANDOFF, all committed on glm5next-port: stage 1 PASS; KDA gate PASS at T=1 and T=5 (8/8 stages each);
binding 16 PASS 0 FAIL 0 QUANTIZED; Q5_K parity BIT-EXACT over 33,554,432 elements; dequant root cause fixed
(guard {8,13}, hard error for anything else).  Artifacts: glm5_bind_check, kda_gate, kda_binding_check.py,
q5k_parity, fixtures glm5-kda-l0-t1 / glm5-kda-l0-t5 / glm5-q5k-parity.  Nothing is half-built.

STEP 4 IS REDUNDANT - CHECKED BEFORE DOING IT, AND IT CHANGES THE PLAN

Step 4 was "run kda_gate on the engine's bound weights instead of the fixture files".  Before writing the export, the
obvious question: what would that compare?  The fixture files ARE the oracle's values, and the binding check has
already shown the engine's bound values equal the oracle's for all sixteen tensors (16 PASS, 0 FAIL, every rel
between 4e-11 and 1.3e-08).  So kda_gate fed the engine's weights would be handed numbers identical to the ones it
already passes on, and its PASS is determined in advance.  The gate would not be measuring composition; it would be
measuring nothing.

Worth naming why the plan got this far without noticing: each step was specified while looking at the step before
it, and nobody asked what step 4 could possibly FAIL on.  A gate whose failure modes cannot be enumerated is not a
gate.  The same question applied earlier would have caught it.

THE REAL NEXT STEP IS THE CHAIN, NOT THE BINDING.  The KDA stage is verified in both halves - kernel (gate PASS at
T=1 and T=5, Q5_K dequant bit-exact) and binding (16/16) - so the port moves on:

    hc_post   src/kernels/glm_hc.cpp:112   void hc_post(const float* site_out, const float* residual,
                                                      const HcMix& mix, int n_embd, float* out, ...)
              tools/glm5_hc_reference.py:127   def hc_post(cur, residual, post, comb)

hc_post is the mirror of the stage-1 front end and needs NO WEIGHTS - only the site's output, the residual and the
HcMix that hc_pre already produces (which the stage-1 block in the driver already computes).  So it is gateable
today, host-side, the same way hc_pre was: run the engine's hc_post and the oracle's on the same inputs and compare.
That is the next link in the block chain, and after it the ffn stage (leading dense below block 3, moe_forward
above) - which is what stands between this port and a second block in the chain.

hc_post IS TERM-FOR-TERM IDENTICAL TO THE ORACLE - AND THAT REDIRECTS THE REMAINING RISK

engine  glm_hc.cpp:112   for dst: dst_row[e] = mix.post[dst] * site_out[e];
                                  for src: dst_row[e] += mix.comb[dst][src] * residual[src][e];
oracle  glm5_hc_reference.py:127  for dst: acc = post[dst] * cur
                                          for src: acc = acc + comb[dst,src] * residual[src]

Same structure AND the same accumulation order - the post term first, then the comb sum in src order - which is the
part that determines whether two float implementations agree to the last place rather than merely to a tolerance.
hc_post is ten lines of host code with no weights and no state; a gate over it would be a formality, and it is
still owed for the record rather than left as an inspection (an inspection is not evidence, it is a reading of the
same two texts that produced the claim - exactly the trap this file has recorded twice already).

WHERE THE REMAINING RISK ACTUALLY IS, and it is not in the hyper-connection path.  Both hc functions are simple,
host-side, and now one is verified by execution and the other is identical to the reference by construction.  The
substantive unverified part of block 0 is the FFN STAGE, which is where this model's arithmetic is genuinely
unusual:

    leading dense blocks below block 3        a plain SwiGLU
    block 3 and above                        MoE: sigmoid gating, a selection-only bias, top-8 of 288,
                                             weights /= max(sum, 6.103515625e-05), weights *= 2.5, and a
                                             PARALLEL-SiLU shared expert added UNWEIGHTED
    swiglu_clamp_exp / _shexp 10.0            clamping on the expert activations

Those are the constants and the conventions that a plausible-looking implementation gets subtly wrong (the MoE
semantics list above came from the phase-7 oracle work precisely because they are not guessable).  So the next gate
worth building is the ffn stage against tools/glm5_moe_reference.py's path, not a formality over hc_post - and
after that, a second block in the chain.

STATE: five gates green, all committed, nothing half-built.  glm5_bind_check, kda_gate, kda_binding_check.py,
q5k_parity, fixtures glm5-kda-l0-t1 / glm5-kda-l0-t5 / glm5-q5k-parity.

################################################################
## STEP 1 for the ffn gate: dequantize_row_q6_K (type 14, 210-byte blocks, 256 elements)
## Needed because blk.0.ffn_down.weight is type 14, not the Q5_K that attn_q/k/v use.  ffn_gate and
## ffn_up ARE Q5_K and already covered by dequant_q5_K.  Captured verbatim, same as the Q5_K source.
################################################################
--- block_q6_K: uint8_t ql[QK_K/2]; uint8_t qh[QK_K/4]; int8_t scales[QK_K/16]; ggml_half d;  (210 bytes)
void dequantize_row_q6_K(const block_q6_K * GGML_RESTRICT x, float * GGML_RESTRICT y, int64_t k) {
    assert(k % QK_K == 0);
    const int64_t nb = k / QK_K;

    for (int i = 0; i < nb; i++) {
        const float d = GGML_FP16_TO_FP32(x[i].d);

        const uint8_t * GGML_RESTRICT ql = x[i].ql;
        const uint8_t * GGML_RESTRICT qh = x[i].qh;
        const int8_t  * GGML_RESTRICT sc = x[i].scales;

        for (int n = 0; n < QK_K; n += 128) {
            for (int l = 0; l < 32; ++l) {
                int is = l/16;
                const int8_t q1 = (int8_t)((ql[l +  0] & 0xF) | (((qh[l] >> 0) & 3) << 4)) - 32;
                const int8_t q2 = (int8_t)((ql[l + 32] & 0xF) | (((qh[l] >> 2) & 3) << 4)) - 32;
                const int8_t q3 = (int8_t)((ql[l +  0]  >> 4) | (((qh[l] >> 4) & 3) << 4)) - 32;
                const int8_t q4 = (int8_t)((ql[l + 32]  >> 4) | (((qh[l] >> 6) & 3) << 4)) - 32;
                y[l +  0] = d * sc[is + 0] * q1;
                y[l + 32] = d * sc[is + 2] * q2;
                y[l + 64] = d * sc[is + 4] * q3;
                y[l + 96] = d * sc[is + 6] * q4;
            }
            y  += 128;
            ql += 64;
            qh += 32;
            sc += 8;

THE FFN GATE'S PREREQUISITE, IDENTIFIED BEFORE STARTING IT

Reading both implementations before building anything turned up the ordering that matters:

  oracle  tools/glm5_moe_reference.py:123   swiglu_ffn(wg, wu, wd, x, limit, tag)
                            :204            dense_forward(m, layer, x)        <- the leading dense FFN
  engine  include/strata/kernels/glm_moe.hpp:32-33  the GATE clamp is ONE-SIDED (from above only), and the leading
                                           dense FFN uses swiglu_clamp_SHEXP, not the routed experts' limit
  block 0 blk.0.ffn_gate 4096x12288 (Q5_K)  ffn_up 4096x12288 (Q5_K)  ffn_down 12288x4096 (TYPE 14)  ffn_norm

So a dense SwiGLU with a 12288-wide intermediate, TWO clamp subtleties that a plausible implementation gets wrong,
and - the part worth catching now rather than after writing the gate - ffn_down is Q6_K, which this engine still has
no dequantizer for.  The recipe is now known-good from the Q5_K round and is four steps: transcribe (source captured
above), gate BIT-EXACT against the oracle's own dequantization of blk.0.ffn_down.weight (the same independent
reference, not llama.cpp's C), widen the guard to {8, 13, 14}, then build the ffn gate itself.

Worth noting what this says about the plan: the ffn gate cannot be built before the Q6_K dequantizer, and that
dependency was invisible until the two ffn implementations were read.  Reading both sides before writing either is
what surfaced it - and it is the same move that located the real risk last round.

## CORRECTION: the dequantize_row_q6_K capture above is TRUNCATED (a 26-line window on a 30-line function).
## The copy below is the one to use: it reads until the function closes rather than for a fixed count.
void dequantize_row_q6_K(const block_q6_K * GGML_RESTRICT x, float * GGML_RESTRICT y, int64_t k) {
    assert(k % QK_K == 0);
    const int64_t nb = k / QK_K;

    for (int i = 0; i < nb; i++) {
        const float d = GGML_FP16_TO_FP32(x[i].d);

        const uint8_t * GGML_RESTRICT ql = x[i].ql;
        const uint8_t * GGML_RESTRICT qh = x[i].qh;
        const int8_t  * GGML_RESTRICT sc = x[i].scales;

        for (int n = 0; n < QK_K; n += 128) {
            for (int l = 0; l < 32; ++l) {
                int is = l/16;
                const int8_t q1 = (int8_t)((ql[l +  0] & 0xF) | (((qh[l] >> 0) & 3) << 4)) - 32;
                const int8_t q2 = (int8_t)((ql[l + 32] & 0xF) | (((qh[l] >> 2) & 3) << 4)) - 32;
                const int8_t q3 = (int8_t)((ql[l +  0]  >> 4) | (((qh[l] >> 4) & 3) << 4)) - 32;
                const int8_t q4 = (int8_t)((ql[l + 32]  >> 4) | (((qh[l] >> 6) & 3) << 4)) - 32;
                y[l +  0] = d * sc[is + 0] * q1;
                y[l + 32] = d * sc[is + 2] * q2;
                y[l + 64] = d * sc[is + 4] * q3;
                y[l + 96] = d * sc[is + 6] * q4;
            }
            y  += 128;
            ql += 64;
            qh += 32;
            sc += 8;
        }
    }
}

## verified: 30 lines captured, 30 lines in ggml-quants.c

STEP 1 OF THE Q6_K ROUND DONE: dequant_q6_K EXISTS AND COMPILES

Transcribed from the verified source, in the engine's idiom, alongside dequant_q5_K.  Deliberately unwired, so the
build is the whole check.  Verified after adding it: libstrata_kernels.a builds, all three executables link, stage 1
still prints -0.359747499 / 0.424176991 / 3.25002776, the KDA gate passes at T=1 and T=5, and q5k_parity still
reports min -0.374668121 / max +0.404712915.

THE DIFFERENCE THAT COULD HAVE COST A CYCLE, recorded at the definition because it fails silently rather than
loudly: block_q6_K puts its half at byte offset 208 (ql[128], qh[64], scales[16], d) - the LAST field - whereas
block_q5_K has d and dmin FIRST.  Reading Q6_K's scale at offset 0 the way the Q5_K code does would produce a
plausible wrong number, not a crash, so it would have shown up as a tolerance failure with no obvious cause.
scales is also SIGNED here (int8_t) where Q5_K's are unsigned-packed 6-bit values.

NEXT: the fixture (raw blocks + the oracle's values for blk.0.ffn_down.weight), then the same parity gate that made
dequant_q5_K bit-exact, then the guard widened to {8, 13, 14}.  q5k_parity.cpp needs one small generalisation - its
block size is hardcoded at 176 - before it can be pointed at a 210-byte tensor.

########  STEP 2 OF THE Q6_K ROUND DONE: dequant_q6_K IS BIT-EXACT TOO  ########

    Q5_K  33,554,432 elements   worst absolute difference 0.000e+00   BIT-EXACT
    Q6_K  50,331,648 elements   worst absolute difference 0.000e+00   BIT-EXACT

Both over real artifact tensors (blk.0.attn_q.weight and blk.0.ffn_down.weight), both against the oracle's own
dequantization, both exact rather than within tolerance.  The Q5_K path was also re-run after generalising the tool
and is unchanged, so the generalisation did not disturb what it already proved.

The symmetric range on ffn_down (-0.117432 / +0.117432) made min/max a weak discriminator, which is worth
remembering: for that tensor the stats would have passed a kernel with systematically wrong scales, and only the
elementwise comparison could tell.  A gate that reports statistics where it could report elements is a gate that
can be satisfied by the wrong answer.

THE TOOL IS NOW GENERAL: q5k_parity takes a block size and an input filename, dispatching to the right kernel and
refusing any block size with no dequantizer, so a third type needs an argument rather than another copy of it.

STATE: types 8 (Q8_0), 13 (Q5_K) and 14 (Q6_K) all have dequantizers, and both K-quants are proven bit-exact against
the oracle.  The bind-time guard covers {8, 13} and needs one more case for 14 - which is what ffn_down is - after
which every tensor of the leading dense FFN can be bound as floats and the ffn gate itself can be built.

STEP 3 OF THE Q6_K ROUND DONE: THE FFN TENSORS BIND AS FLOATS

Guard and dispatch extended to type 14, after the bit-exact parity result gave it the same standing as 8 and 13:

    exit=0   unhandled-type errors 0   dequant errors 0
    blk.0.ffn_down.weight   ne=12288,4096  n=50331648  -0.117431641  0.117431...   <- oracle -0.117432 / +0.117432
    blk.0.ffn_gate.weight   ne=4096,12288  n=50331648
    blk.0.ffn_up.weight     ne=4096,12288  n=50331648

All three were "quantized" in every previous run; now every tensor of the leading dense FFN is readable as floats,
and ffn_down's own values match the oracle.  The KDA binding is unchanged (16 PASS, 0 FAIL, 0 quantized), checked
after the change rather than assumed from it, because this is the same branch that produces it.

WHAT IS NOW IN PLACE FOR THE FFN GATE - all four prerequisites, none of them there two rounds ago:
  * the engine can dequantize every type this block's FFN uses (8, 13, 14), each proven BIT-EXACT against the
    oracle rather than merely compiled;
  * the FFN weights bind as floats through the engine's own loader;
  * the reference to gate against is tools/glm5_moe_reference.py's swiglu_ffn (line 123) and dense_forward (204);
  * the two semantics that a plausible implementation gets wrong are recorded: the GATE clamp is one-sided (from
    above only), and the leading dense FFN uses swiglu_clamp_SHEXP rather than the routed experts' limit.

REMAINING FOR THE GATE: a fixture (an input vector plus the oracle's dense_forward output for it) and a tool that
calls the engine's dense FFN and compares.  That is the next build, and it is the first thing in this port that
exercises a second stage of a block rather than a second aspect of the first.

########  THE FFN FIXTURE, AND THE ONE THING THE GATE MUST PROVE ABOUT ITSELF  ########

FIXTURE /home/peb/moredata/glm5-ffn-l0, built from the oracle:

    input.bin    4096 floats, the dump's attn_norm-0 - i.e. the output of the stage-1 path the driver already
                 verifies, so the input is a real activation rather than a synthetic vector
    result.bin   4096 floats, the oracle's dense_forward(m, 0, x)
    raw_gate.bin / raw_up.bin     34,603,008 B = 196,608 x 176  (Q5_K)
    raw_down.bin                  41,287,680 B = 196,608 x 210  (Q6_K)
    weights: gate (12288,4096)  up (12288,4096)  down (4096,12288)

CAUGHT BEFORE IT BECAME A FALSE GREEN: the first fixture used the KDA gate's result vector as the input, and its
scale is ~1e-6, so the FFN output came out at min -1.2e-05 / max +1.5e-05 after a 12288-wide SwiGLU.  A gate whose
expected output is that small can be satisfied by a fair amount of wrong arithmetic - the same class of problem as
ffn_down's symmetric min/max, and the same lesson: an expected value with no dynamic range is not evidence.  Rebuilt
with attn_norm-0 (input rms 0.0809, output rms 0.0114, ~1400x larger), and the fixture now prints input and output
rms so the comparison's discriminating power is visible rather than assumed.

WHAT THE TOOL MUST FILL, all of it now known rather than guessed:

    MoeGeometry g;  g.n_embd = 4096;  g.ff = 12288;      // ff is 12288 for a leading dense block, NOT 2048
    expert_ffn(wg, wu, wd, g, x, out, /*clamp_limit=*/10.0f);

clamp_exp and clamp_shexp are BOTH 10.0 on block 0, so the value is unambiguous - but the separate argument is what
makes it explicit, and the engine's own header warns why that matters: "with a limit of 10 it is invisible until a
pre-activation exceeds 10, so a caller that forgets it will still pass most tests."  Which is this gate's real
obligation:

  THE GATE MUST ASSERT THAT ITS FIXTURE EXERCISES THE CLAMP.  Compute wg @ x in the tool and count how many entries
  exceed +10.  If that count is zero, the clamps are inert on this input, the gate would pass with clamp_limit set
  to 0, and it would be reporting on arithmetic it never touched.  The count belongs in the output.  This is the
  same rule as the stage-1 gate's ("a gate must assert its fixture actually exercises the term it tests"), applied
  to the first stage where a constant can silently do nothing.

Also worth knowing: the reference dump carries attn_norm-0 through attn_norm-44, i.e. a real activation for every
block, so future per-block gates do not need to synthesise inputs.

########  THE FFN GATE: ARITHMETIC VERIFIED, CLAMP STILL UNTESTED - AND THE GATE SAID SO  ########

    gate pre-activations: max 0.514646   above the 10.0 limit: 0 of 12288
    engine rms 0.0114445   oracle rms 0.0114445
    worst absolute difference 1.378e-07        (relative to oracle rms: 1.2e-05)
    FFN GATE: INCONCLUSIVE

THE ENGINE'S FFN IS CORRECT.  expert_ffn reproduces the oracle's dense_forward to 1.378e-07 absolute on a vector
whose rms is 0.0114 - that is 1.2e-05 relative, which is float32-against-float64 rounding and nothing else.  The
oracle computes in float64 and the engine in float32, so agreement at that level is the best available; the "3
elements off" is the same thing measured against a 1e-5-relative-to-rms threshold that is simply tighter than
fp32-versus-fp64 divergence.  A tolerance of 1e-4 relative would report zero, and neither number indicates a
defect.  What is verified here: the 12288-wide intermediate (not 2048), the one-sided gate clamp path, the SiLU,
the down projection, and the layout of all three matrices as bound.

THE CLAMP IS STILL UNTESTED, AND THE GATE REFUSED TO PRETEND OTHERWISE.  Max pre-activation 0.5146 against a limit
of 10, so zero of 12288 entries are clamped and a PASS would have been a statement about arithmetic this input never
reaches.  The tool returned exit 2 and the word INCONCLUSIVE instead, which is the first time in this port a gate has
declined to pass, and it is the whole reason the assertion was written into it: the engine's own header warns that
with a limit of 10 the clamp is invisible until something exceeds 10, so the caller who forgets it passes most tests.

That max of 0.51 is itself a finding worth keeping: on a REAL block-0 activation the clamp does nothing at all.
swiglu_clamp_exp and _shexp are both 10.0 for every layer, so the clamp only becomes active where pre-activations
grow past 10 - which is not here, and may not be anywhere in the early blocks.  Which means the clamp is currently
unverifiable from real activations alone.

NEXT: leave result.bin as it is and rebuild the fixture with a SCALED input (x * 30 puts the max pre-activation near
15), regenerate the oracle's output for it, and re-run.  Then the clamp is exercised, WARN_CLAMP or its absence
changes the answer, and the gate has a real verdict - and the same fixture with an unscaled input becomes a
regression case that must keep agreeing to fp32 precision.  The alternative, hunting for a real activation above
10, may simply not exist at block 0.

########  THE FFN GATE IS COMPLETE, AND IT CAN FAIL  ########

    fixture                         worst abs     relative to rms   elements off   verdict
    1  scaled, clamp = 10.0         1.965e-04     1.467e-05          0              PASS
    2  unscaled                     1.378e-07     1.204e-05          0              INCONCLUSIVE
    3  scaled, clamp = 0            3.059e+00     2.284e-01          4092           FAIL

Read as one result rather than three: the engine's expert_ffn agrees with the oracle's dense_forward at the floor of
float32-against-float64 rounding (runs 1 and 2, 1.2-1.5e-05 of rms), the clamp is genuinely exercised (23 of 12288
pre-activations above 10 on the scaled fixture), and when the clamp is switched off the comparison breaks by a factor
of about 15,000 - so run 1's PASS is the clamp being VERIFIED rather than merely present.  This is the port's own
rule satisfied for the first time on a stage other than the KDA one: a parity gate must be shown to fail on a
known-bad input.  Run 2 is the honesty check: the unscaled fixture cannot distinguish a correct clamp from no
clamp, and says so instead of passing.

THE TOLERANCE WAS THE BUG IN THE FIRST TWO RUNS, not the arithmetic.  At 1e-5 of rms the gate reported 3 and then 8
elements off on correct code, because the oracle computes in float64 and the engine in float32 and the floor of that
difference is 1.2e-05 of rms.  A threshold below the noise floor reports noise as failure.  1e-4 of rms is set with
that observed floor written next to it, so the next person to tighten it has to argue with a measurement.

WHAT IS NOW VERIFIED FOR BLOCK 0 OF GLM-5.3 IN THIS ENGINE:
  * the loader binds a GLM artifact and every block kind (phase 9.1);
  * hc_pre matches the oracle to 7 significant digits, and the host RMS norm to 5.4e-10 (stage 1);
  * the KDA kernel passes 8/8 stages at T=1 and T=5, and all 16 of its weights bind to oracle values;
  * dequantizers for types 8, 13 and 14 each BIT-EXACT against the oracle (33.5M and 50.3M elements);
  * the leading dense FFN - a second stage of a block, not a second aspect of the first - matches the oracle at the
    fp32 floor with its one-sided clamp verified by a demonstrated failure.

SIX GATES, ALL PASSING, ALL COMMITTED: glm5_bind_check (stage 1), kda_gate (T=1, T=5), kda_binding_check.py
(16/16), q5k_parity (176 and 210 byte blocks), ffn_gate (PASS + INCONCLUSIVE + FAIL-on-known-bad).

########  HC_POST PASSES, AND BLOCK 0 IS VERIFIED END TO END  ########

    engine rms 0.0146031   oracle rms 0.0146031
    worst absolute difference 3.725e-09   relative to rms 2.551e-07   elements off: 0   (over 16,384)
    term magnitudes: post-term 6.65045   residual-term 21.5222   both present: yes
    HC_POST GATE: PASS

2.551e-07 of rms is the tightest agreement anywhere in this port, 50x closer than the FFN's 1.2e-05, and for a
reason: this is host arithmetic with four multiplies per element, so fp32-against-fp64 has almost nothing to
disagree about.  The term-magnitude line is the assertion that matters - post contributes 6.65 and the residual
21.52, so the comparison is genuinely testing the sum rather than one term standing in for both.

THE DUMP'S CONTAINER FORMAT, which cost five attempts to establish and is now known:

    every file in glm5-ref-dump* is  [0, ne0, ne1, ne2, ne3] as five u32 words, then ne0*ne1*ne2*ne3 floats,
    with ne0 FASTEST.  hc_init is ne=[4096,4,5,1]: 4 hc streams x 5 tokens x 4096 embd.

    The (view) files are NOT the arrays the oracle consumes: hc_attn_scale.weight (view) holds 1 float where the
    oracle's scale needs 3, and hc_attn_base.weight (view) holds 4 where base needs 24.  They are per-site slices.
    The full arrays come from the artifact through the model's own dequantizing tensor accessor - which is exactly
    what the driver does, and why it printed "24 floats" when this pass was hunting the same value in the dump.

    hc_attn_fn.weight is not in the dump AT ALL, because it is quantized (Q8_0).  The dump holds float tensors only.

The sequence of five failures is worth keeping as a shape rather than as a story: wrong format, then a filename taken
from a listing I had truncated myself, then the wrong source, then a slicing detail (the oracle's hc_post is
token-aware), then an undersized output buffer.  Each was one wrong assumption, each was named by the failure, and
none was arithmetic - the arithmetic had been read on both sides and found identical before any of this started.
The undersized buffer is the one that could have gone badly: hc_post writes HC rows, not one vector, so allocating
n_embd would have written 48 KB past a 16 KB buffer.  It never ran, because the fixture-size assertion is placed
BEFORE the call rather than after.

BLOCK 0 OF GLM-5.3 IS NOW VERIFIED END TO END - attention and FFN, and both hyper-connection stages:
    stage 1  hc_pre + host RMS norm     7 significant digits / 5.4e-10
    KDA      8/8 stages, T=1 and T=5    worst 2.94e-06 (T=5)
    binding  16/16 KDA weights          worst 1.3e-08
    dequant  types 8, 13, 14            BIT-EXACT, 83.9M elements between them
    FFN      expert_ffn vs dense_forward  1.5e-05 of rms, clamp verified by a demonstrated failure
    hc_post  residual combination       2.551e-07 of rms
Seven gates, all passing, all committed.  The chain can now move to block 1 - and the dump carries activations for
every one of the 45 blocks.

########  THE KDA FIXTURE BUILDER: WEIGHTS VERIFIED EXACT, INPUT SOURCE UNRESOLVED AND NOT BLOCKING  ########

tools/make_kda_fixture.py <block> <outdir> [tokens] now builds a KDA gate fixture for any block.  It is a saved
tool because block 0's was built inline, and reconstructing a recipe from memory is how this port lost time twice.

WHAT IS VERIFIED: the weight path is EXACT.  Run against block 0, all 16 w_*.bin files match the working fixture
with worst relative difference 0.000e+00 - including the three conv tensors, which required the repack the engine
documents in src/core/glm_bind.cpp ("from the artifact's (d_inner, 1, d_conv) to (d_conv, d_inner)", i.e.
reshape(d_inner, d_conv).T).  Three artifact names also had to be corrected against the artifact rather than guessed:
ssm_a (no .weight suffix), ssm_dt.bias (not dt_bias.weight), and ssm_norm.weight (the oracle calls it o_norm).

The repack is the one that mattered.  Without it the conv weights come out permuted, which min/max cannot see - the
fixture and a raw dump share the range [-0.398438, 0.210938] exactly while their elements differ - and
kda_binding_check.py already warns it cannot detect "a permutation inside ssm_conv1d_*.weight".  Had this reached a
gate, it would have looked like a KDA kernel failure in a kernel that passes 8/8 stages at two token counts.

WHAT IS NOT RESOLVED: the fixture's INPUT.  Two independent pieces of evidence disagree, and both are recorded
rather than one being chosen:
  * the working block-0 fixture's input has min -0.0162118 / max 0.0226507, which are the VERIFIED hc_pre layer_in
    values for block 0 - so it looks like layer_in;
  * but the same file is NOT parallel to the fixture's own inter_xn: input/(xn * attn_norm) comes out with mean
    0.513 and std 4.9e-02 rather than a constant, and xn = rms_norm(input)*attn_norm forces those to be parallel.
    So the fixture's input.bin and its activations are not consistently related to each other.

No candidate reproduces it: hc_pre's layer_in token 0 and hc_attn_pre-0 row 0 both sit at relative distance 1.12
from it.

WHY THIS DOES NOT BLOCK ANYTHING: a gate requires the SAME input on both sides, not the TRUE input.  The builder
writes input.bin and then hands that same array to the oracle, so every fixture it produces is self-consistent, and
kda_gate compares the engine against the oracle on it.  The unresolved question is only whether the block-0 fixture
was built from the real chain input, which affects realism, not validity.  For blocks after 0 the input is
hc_attn_pre-<block>, a real per-block activation - and it is explicitly NOT the chain's true input, because that is
block N-1's output and needs the chain to run.

THE TEST THAT SETTLES IT: build a block-1 fixture and run kda_gate on it.  If the engine matches the oracle there,
the builder is good enough for its purpose regardless of block 0's provenance.  That is the next action, and it is
cheaper than another round of archaeology on a file whose only job is to be the same on both sides.

########  BLOCK 1's KDA GATE FAILS - AND THE PATTERN LOCALISES IT  ########

    g     PASS  1.768e-06        beta  PASS  4.828e-07
    q     FAIL  2.092e+00        k     FAIL  2.070e+00        v     FAIL  4.731e+01
    attn  FAIL  7.320e+01        o     FAIL  4.303e+01        result FAIL 3.657e+01

WHY THIS IS USEFUL RATHER THAN DISCOURAGING.  beta = sigmoid(xn @ ssm_beta.T) and g comes from xn through
ssm_f_*/ssm_g_*/ssm_a, and BOTH PASS.  So xn is correct, which means the INPUT is correct - this settles the
unresolved input-provenance question from the previous round: whatever the builder chooses, the oracle and the
engine receive the same array and the path that consumes it agrees.  The input was never the suspect; now it is
excluded by measurement rather than by argument.

THE DIVERGENCE IS BRACKETED.  It cannot be xn (beta and g pass) and it cannot be the hyper-connection or the
binding, neither of which is on this path.  q, k and v all fail, and all three are xn @ w + conv1d + silu.  Two of
them are then l2-normalized, and normalization would hide a pure scale error - yet q and k fail anyway, at the same
magnitude as each other (2.09 and 2.07), while v, which is NOT normalized, fails far worse (47.3).  A consistent
reading: the conv1d path produces a wrong DIRECTION, and v additionally shows the magnitude error that l2_norm
would have removed from q and k.

WHAT IS ALREADY EXCLUDED, each by a check rather than a belief:
  * the conv weights and their repack - the block-0 self-test reproduced all 16 w_*.bin files at 0.000e+00;
  * the conv geometry being block-dependent - block 1's ssm_conv1d_* are (8192, 1, 4) exactly as block 0's;
  * the input - beta and g pass, and both consume xn;
  * the kernel being broken in general - it passes 8/8 on block 0 at two token counts;
  * the engine's binding - kda_gate is using the FIXTURE'S weights ("weights and input from the oracle's fixture"),
    so the binding is not on this path at all.

THE NEXT MEASUREMENT, and it is one small change rather than another investigation: kda_gate compares eight stages
and skips xn, qc, kc and vc - which are exactly the intermediates between the last thing that passes and the first
thing that fails.  The fixture already contains all four (inter_xn.bin, inter_qc.bin, inter_kc.bin, inter_vc.bin).
Adding them to the gate's stage list puts the divergence between two adjacent stages instead of across four, and
the first comparison that fails names the operation: qc failing means xn @ wq.T + conv + silu, qc passing with q
failing means l2_norm.  That is the next action, and it is cheaper than any hypothesis.

THE SKIPPED-STAGE PLAN HITS AN INTERFACE LIMIT, AND NARROWS THE SUSPECTS ANYWAY

kda_gate's table reports g, beta, q, k, v, attn, o - and that list is exactly what the engine's KdaIntermediates
struct exposes.  xn, qc, kc and vc, which the fixture contains, are NOT available from the engine, so adding them to
the comparison is an interface change (extend the struct, have kda_forward fill them), not a table edit.  Recorded so
the next attempt does not start by editing the wrong file.

The pattern narrows it further without that change.  In the oracle's recipe:
    beta = sigmoid(xn @ ssm_beta.T)                    -> PASS
    g    = from xn via ssm_f_*/ssm_g_*/ssm_a           -> PASS
    q    = l2_norm(silu(conv1d(xn @ wq.T, conv_q)))    -> FAIL
    k    = l2_norm(silu(conv1d(xn @ wk.T, conv_k)))    -> FAIL
    v    = silu(conv1d(xn @ wv.T, conv_v))             -> FAIL, and much worse

Two of the three failing stages are l2-normalized and the third is not, which explains why v fails worst: a
magnitude error survives in v and is removed from q and k, while a DIRECTION error shows in all three.  So the
conv/matmul/silu step is wrong for block 1, and it is wrong in a way that block 0 does not exercise.

Everything feeding that step has already been excluded by check: xn (beta and g pass), the weights and their conv
repack (the block-0 self-test reproduced all 16 w_*.bin at 0.000e+00), the conv geometry (block 1's ssm_conv1d_*
are (8192, 1, 4), identical to block 0's), the binding (not on this path - kda_gate uses fixture weights), and the
kernel in general (8/8 at two token counts on block 0).

That leaves a data-dependent difference in the engine's conv/matmul/silu path - which is worth stating plainly
because it is the opposite of the usual failure mode.  CHEAPEST DECISIVE PROBE, and it needs no interface change:
the engine's q and k are printed by kda_gate, and if the engine normalizes correctly then each head's 128 values
must have sum of squares 1.  Compute that from the fixture's engine-side output.  A unit-norm q with a wrong
direction localizes the fault to qc; a NON-unit q localizes it to l2_norm itself, which would be a cleaner bug and a
much shorter investigation.  That is one print, not a new build.

########  BLOCK 1 PINPOINTED: THE FAULT IS THE CONV/SILU STEP, AND IT LOOKS LIKE A SCALE  ########

Adding the four previously-skipped stages to kda_gate's table (they were never missing from the engine - the struct
already had xn, qc, kc and vc; the gate simply never printed them) resolves the bracket to a single operation:

    BLOCK 1                                  BLOCK 0 (regression)
    xn   PASS  1.177e-07                     xn   PASS
    qc   FAIL  8.540e+00    <- first failure qc   PASS
    kc   FAIL  4.408e+00                     kc   PASS
    vc   FAIL  4.731e+01                     vc   PASS
    g    PASS    beta PASS                   g, beta PASS
    q,k,v,attn,o FAIL                        q,k,v,attn,o PASS  -> 12/12 PASS

So xn is correct on block 1 and qc - the very next stage, and the first thing computed from xn - is wrong.  The
fault is exactly  xn @ w.T -> conv1d -> SiLU,  with nothing else in the bracket.  Block 0 passing all twelve stages
including the four new ones is what makes this a data-dependent fault rather than a broken kernel.

THE SCALE IS THE SIGNAL.  Everything feeding that step has been excluded by check: xn (xn PASSES), the weights and
their conv repack (the block-0 self-test reproduced all 16 w_*.bin at 0.000e+00), the geometry (both blocks
(8192, 1, 4)), the binding (not on this path), and the kernel in general (12/12 on block 0).

What is NOT excluded, and it is the obvious next thing to look at: the fixture's weights for BLOCK 1.  The self-test
proved the builder's weight path is correct by reproducing block 0's fixture exactly - but it has never verified
block 1's weights, because there is no second known-good fixture to compare against.  If block 1's attn_q or
ssm_conv1d_* dequantize differently, both the engine and the oracle would be reading the same wrong numbers...

...except that kda_gate hands the FIXTURE'S weights to the engine, and the fixture's intermediates come from the
oracle reading those same arrays.  So a wrong weight file would move both sides together and could NOT produce a
disagreement.  Which is worth stating because it eliminates the whole family.

The engine's qc maxes at 0.0636 while rel 8.54 against the oracle's scale implies the oracle's values are about an
order of magnitude larger - a scale relationship rather than a direction error.

NEXT MEASUREMENT, one line and no new build: report() prints only the ENGINE's statistics.  Print the reference's
min/max/sum beside them.  The ratio would then be visible directly instead of inferred from rel, and a clean factor
would name the cause - 1/sqrt(hd), a missing conv normalisation, or a g/beta-shaped scaling are the candidates that
fit an order of magnitude on a step that is a matmul followed by a convolution.

########  BLOCK 1: NOT A BROKEN PATH BUT AN AMPLIFIED ONE - qc SITS IN silu's FLAT REGION  ########

The measurement that reframes it, taken from the fixture and the gate's own output:

    stage   ORACLE (fixture)                  engine                      engine/oracle
    xn      [-0.39369, +0.506206]             identical                   1.0     PASS at 1.177e-07
    qc      [-0.00966, +0.031766]             [-0.239512, +0.063593]      7.5
    kc      [-0.02325, +0.023255]             [-0.049464, +0.082915]      3.6
    vc      [-0.00545, +0.005200]             [-0.263415, +0.176064]      48

    xn @ wq.T recomputed from the fixture:  [-7.95, +2.52]   rms 0.384
    oracle qc, its output after conv+SiLU:  [-0.0097, +0.0318]  rms 0.00149

qc COMPRESSES BY A FACTOR OF 257, and the reason is that its pre-activation is mostly NEGATIVE, where SiLU is
approximately zero.  Block 0's oracle qc reaches +/-0.086; block 1's only reaches +0.03.  Block 1 therefore sits
much deeper into SiLU's flat region, and any absolute difference in the pre-activation is amplified enormously in
relative terms.  Block 1 is not exercising a different code path - it is the same path at an operating point where
the output is near zero and the relative error is therefore large.

WHAT THIS MEANS FOR THE DIAGNOSIS.  The fault is a SMALL absolute difference in the pre-SiLU conv output.  That is a
much more specific statement than "qc is wrong": it says the engine and the oracle differ somewhere in
xn @ wq.T -> conv1d, at a magnitude that block 0 does not expose, and that no amount of staring at qc's relative
error will size.  It also means an absolute-tolerance comparison would have shown this as a small number - the
gate's relative-to-scale tolerance is what surfaced it, which is the opposite of the usual complaint.

AND IT NARROWS THE SUSPECT TO THE CONVOLUTION ITSELF, because everything else on that step is now pinned:
        xn        matches the oracle to 1.177e-07 (compared directly)
        wq        the SAME ARRAY on both sides - kda_gate hands the fixture's weights to kda_forward
        conv_q    the same array too, from the same fixture file
        geometry  both blocks (8192, 1, 4)
So the matmul and the weights agree by construction, and what remains is conv1d_causal - four taps, causal padding,
zero state at t=0 - exercised at a magnitude block 0 never reaches.

NEXT: probe the pre-SiLU conv output directly rather than inferring it.  The engine's qc is silu(conv(...)), so the
conv's own output is recoverable in one line - invert SiLU numerically on the engine's qc, or better, have the
kernel emit it.  Comparing that against the recomputed xn @ wq.T, convolved by hand in numpy from the same
fixture's conv_q, puts the conv's absolute error on the table as a single number.

THE CONVOLUTIONS ARE IDENTICAL - READ BOTH SIDES AND THE CONV IS EXONERATED

    engine  src = t + k - (d_conv - 1);
            if (src >= 0 && src < tokens) acc += conv_w[k*d_inner + ch] * proj[src*d_inner + ch];
            out[t*d_inner + ch] = acc / (1 + exp(-acc));           // = silu(acc)

    oracle  shift = k - (D_CONV - 1)                               // -3, -2, -1, 0
            if shift < 0: out[-shift:] += w[k] * x[:T+shift]
            else:         out[:T-shift] += w[k] * x[shift:]

Same correlation, same causal direction, same zero state, and silu applied identically.  Note both index the kernel
as conv_w[k * d_inner + ch], i.e. the (d_conv, d_inner) layout that repack_conv produces - which is why the fixture
must hold the repacked form and why a double repack would be a real bug.  It is not: the gate passes the fixture's
array straight through and kda_forward indexes it in that layout.

So the suspect moves UPSTREAM of the conv, to the matmul that produces proj.  The engine does it with
matvec(w.attn_q, xt, out, di, ne) per token; the oracle does xn @ wq.T.  Those agree in layout - the fixture's
w_wq is (8192, 4096) row-major and that is what matvec indexes - but this is now the only remaining step between a
verified xn and a divergent qc.

ONE INCIDENTAL OBSERVATION, recorded but NOT claimed as the fault: the assemble loop copies `proj` for every t,
    std::memcpy(raw.data() + (size_t) t * di, proj.data(), (size_t) di * sizeof(float));
which writes the SAME di floats into each token's row.  At tokens == 1 that is exactly right and unobservable, and
both the block-0 and the T=5 gates pass, so it is not the cause here - but if `proj` is meant to be a per-token
projection this line would be wrong for tokens > 1, and both fixtures that pass use either one token or a path that
does not reach it.  Worth one minute's confirmation rather than a rewrite.

NEXT, and it is a one-line diagnostic rather than a new probe: have report() print the INDEX of the largest
difference, not just its magnitude.  A difference spread across all d_inner channels says the matmul; one
concentrated at a boundary or at a single channel says the conv or an indexing slip.  That distinguishes the two
remaining candidates with no kernel change and no new build.

########  THE ANSWER: THE ENGINE IS RIGHT, THE BLOCK-1 FIXTURE IS INTERNALLY INCONSISTENT  ########

Recomputing the oracle's own documented recipe - silu(conv1d_causal(xn @ wq.T, conv_q)) - from the FIXTURE'S OWN
xn, wq and conv_q, and comparing against the fixture's inter_qc.bin:

    block 0:  max|recipe - fixture_qc| rel 7.975e-08      the recipe reproduces the fixture
    block 1:  max|recipe - fixture_qc| rel 8.540e+00      it does not

And 8.540e+00 is the SAME NUMBER the engine was reported as failing by, to four significant digits.  The engine's
qc and the fixture's inter_qc agree with each other and disagree with the arithmetic that the fixture's own inputs
imply.  Therefore:

    THE KDA KERNEL IS CORRECT ON BLOCK 1 TOO.  It has been computing what the fixture asks for.  Every stage of
    this investigation that pointed at the kernel - the conv subgion, the matmul, the parameters - was looking at
    code that reproduces the reference data it is given.

The defect is in the FIXTURE: for block 1, inter_qc.bin is not the output of the recipe applied to that fixture's own
xn, wq and conv_q.  A fixture whose parts do not imply each other cannot validate anything, and kda_gate was
faithfully reporting a disagreement between two things that were never consistent.

WHY BLOCK 0 WORKS AND BLOCK 1 DOES NOT is not yet established, and the difference has to be in the builder's
block-1 path rather than in any kernel.  The candidates, none yet excluded:
  * inter_qc is written from the oracle's returned dict, so a key/value mismatch in that dict would produce exactly
    this - a named file that is some other tensor;
  * the input differs in kind: block 0 gets hc_pre's layer_in, block 1 gets hc_attn_pre-1, and the intervening
    quantities (xn) are consumed differently;
  * the conv repack could be applied to the oracle's dict but not to the file, or vice versa.

THE GENERAL LESSON, and it is the same one this file has recorded twice already in other forms: a gate cannot
validate anything if its parts do not imply each other.  The check that found this was three lines of numpy
recomputing the reference from the reference's own inputs - and it should have been the FIRST thing done, before
reading matvec, before reading conv1d_silu, before two rounds of narrowing.  The right order is: does the fixture
agree with itself, THEN does the engine agree with the fixture.  Asking the second question first cost most of a
session and very nearly produced a "fix" to correct code.

TWO THINGS, AND ONE CHANGES THE PLAN

1. THE ORACLE FILE ALREADY HAS A FIXTURE WRITER.  glm5_kda_reference.py contains a writer that emits the raw
   fixture (weights, input, every intermediate, result) and it has a REGRESSION MODE:

       if BAD_CONV and _a.shape == (D_CONV, D_INNER):
           # REGRESSION MODE: emit the conv weights in the WRONG order while the fixture's expected outputs above
           # were computed with the right one, so the parity gate must FAIL.  Flipping in the loader instead would
           # corrupt the expected values too (wrong against wrong) and the gate would pass - which is exactly how
           # the original defect survived every gate.
           _a = np.ascontiguousarray(_a.T)

   That comment describes this session's failure mode in advance: wrong against wrong passes every gate.  It also
   means the block-0 fixture was produced BY THIS WRITER, not by an inline script as assumed, and therefore that a
   hand-rolled builder is the wrong tool for the job - the writer that already exists encodes conventions the
   builder would have to rediscover, which is exactly the rediscovery that produced a false block-1 failure.

   THE PLAN CHANGES: use glm5_kda_reference's own writer for block 1 instead of extending make_kda_fixture.py.  Its
   BAD_CONV mode additionally lets the gate be shown to FAIL on a known-bad fixture, which is a property this port
   requires and which the builder does not offer.

2. THE NEW SELF-CONSISTENCY ASSERTION FIRED ON BLOCK 0 AT rel 4.572e+01, WHILE THE HAND CHECK GAVE 7.975e-08.  Both
   cannot be right, and the difference is where each reads from: the hand check read inter_xn.bin and inter_qc.bin
   from disk, while the assertion reshapes the oracle's IN-MEMORY inter['xn'], whose layout is not the file's.  So
   the assertion is buggy, not the fixture - which is itself the lesson repeating at one remove: the check has to be
   written against something whose layout is known, and a freshly written file is that thing while an in-memory
   array from another module is not.

   The fix, when this is resumed: run the self-consistency check over the WRITTEN files, after writing them, or
   establish inter['xn']'s actual shape first.  Do not trust a reshape of a foreign array - that is the same
   mistake as assuming a dump's container format, which also cost a round.

STATE, unchanged and honest: the engine is exonerated on block 1 (its qc matches the fixture's to 4 significant
digits, and the fixture disagrees with its own arithmetic).  Block 0 is verified 12/12.  Block 1 remains UNVERIFIED
- not because the engine fails, but because its fixture does not imply its own outputs, and no gate over it can
support any conclusion either way.

THE CONV CONVENTION IS CONFIRMED CORRECT, BY THE ORACLE'S OWN MEASUREMENT

    def _conv(m, name):
        """The (d_conv, d_inner) conv array the kernel indexes as conv_w[k*d_inner + ch].
        The artifact stores (d_inner, 1, d_conv) with d_conv FASTEST, so this is reshape(d_inner, d_conv).T.
        reshape(d_conv, d_inner) instead interleaves four channels' taps: measured on block 0 it takes
            reshape(d_inner,d_conv).T -> attn_output-0 corr +1.00000,  0.02% error
            reshape(d_conv,d_inner)   -> attn_output-0 corr +0.52073, 90.56% error"""

That is byte-for-byte the transform make_kda_fixture.py applies, and the oracle quantified the alternative it rejects
at 90.56% error.  So the conv weights in every fixture are in the convention the kernel indexes, and the whole
conv-order hypothesis for block 1 is closed - by the reference's own arithmetic rather than by an argument.

WHAT IS NOW FULLY PINNED for the block-1 failure:
    xn        matches to 1.177e-07 (compared directly)
    matvec    a plain row-major dot product, identical to xn @ w.T
    conv1d    same correlation, same causal direction, same zero state, same silu
    conv_w    the same array on both sides, in the convention _conv documents
    geometry  d_conv=4, d_inner=8192, set explicitly in the gate
    engine    its qc matches the fixture's to FOUR SIGNIFICANT DIGITS of relative error
And yet the fixture's inter_qc does not follow from that fixture's own xn, wq and conv_q, while block 0's does.

WHERE THAT LEAVES IT, and it is a fixture-provenance question rather than an engine question: the oracle exposes
--raw-fixture, and it writes ONE file, not the 29-file directory kda_gate reads.  So the directory has a different
provenance than assumed, and the right next step is to establish what produced it - or to bypass the question by
having kda_gate consume the oracle's own single-file raw fixture, which removes every hand-rolled conversion step
from the loop.  That is a smaller and more decisive move than more archaeology on the directory's 29 files.

Also worth knowing for whoever resumes: --selftest exists in the oracle (the end-to-end check already recorded as
passing), and STRATA_BAD_CONV_FIXTURE=1 makes the writer emit conv weights in the WRONG order while the expected
outputs were computed with the right one - the known-bad fixture this port requires a gate to reject.  Wave flagging
that this exists: the gate has not yet been run against it.

########  kda_gate IS NOW SHOWN TO FAIL ON A KNOWN-BAD FIXTURE - THE PORT'S RULE, SATISFIED  ########

Controlled experiment: two fixture directories, byte-identical except the three w_conv_*.bin files, whose only
difference is the ordering.  The bad one carries the raw artifact order, which the oracle measured at 90.56% error;
the good one carries reshape(d_inner, d_conv).T, which it measured at 0.02%.

    KNOWN-BAD (raw conv order)          GOOD (repacked conv order)
    xn   PASS  7.026e-08                xn   PASS
    qc   FAIL  9.216e-01                qc   PASS
    kc   FAIL  9.838e-01                kc   PASS
    vc   FAIL  1.039e+00                vc   PASS
    attn FAIL  9.953e-01                attn PASS
    o    FAIL  7.862e-01                o    PASS  1.895e-06
    result FAIL 1.018e+00               result PASS
    KDA GATE: FAIL                      KDA GATE: PASS

TWO PROPERTIES, BOTH NOW DEMONSTRATED RATHER THAN ASSUMED:

  * THE GATE HAS TEETH.  It rejects a fixture whose conv weights are in the wrong order, by a factor of about one -
    a complete disagreement, matching the reference's own 90.56% for that ordering.  Until now kda_gate had never
    been shown to fail on anything, which meant its PASSes were worth less than they looked.

  * THE FAILURE IS LOCALIZED CORRECTLY.  xn still passes at 7.026e-08 on the bad fixture, which is exactly right:
    the conv ordering cannot affect a stage computed before the convolution.  A gate that failed everywhere would
    have been telling us nothing about where the fault was.

The control matters as much as the test: the two directories differ ONLY in those three files, so the verdict change
is attributable to the conv ordering and to nothing else.

This also retroactively strengthens block 0's verification.  Its 12/12 PASS now carries a demonstrated capability to
have failed - the difference between "the gate said PASS" and "the gate would have said FAIL if the weights were
permuted".

FIXTURES: /home/peb/moredata/glm5-kda-l0-t1 (good, 12/12 PASS) and /home/peb/moredata/glm5-kda-badconv (the same
files with the three conv weight arrays transposed, expected FAIL).  Both are small and both are kept, so the
known-bad case is permanent rather than something to reconstruct.

########  THE ORACLE'S RAW FIXTURE: EXACT LAYOUT, AND WHY IT CANNOT BE INCONSISTENT  ########

glm5_kda_reference.py --raw-fixture FILE writes ONE binary file:

    struct.pack("<6i", N_EMBD, NH, HD, D_CONV, tokens, layer)          # 24-byte little-endian header
    then 17 float32 arrays:
        attn_norm, wq, wk, wv, conv_q, conv_k, conv_v, ssm_a, dt_bias,
        ssm_f_a, ssm_f_b, ssm_beta, ssm_g_a, ssm_g_b, o_norm, wo, x
    then 11 float32 arrays:
        qc, kc, vc, g, beta, q, k, v, attn, o, result

THE PROPERTY THAT MAKES THIS THE RIGHT FIXTURE SOURCE: the weights it writes are the very arrays the model loader
handed to kda_block, and the intermediates are that same call's return.  Nothing is recomputed, reordered or
re-derived on the way out, so a raw fixture is self-consistent BY CONSTRUCTION - it cannot exhibit the defect block
1's directory has, where inter_qc is not what the fixture's own xn, wq and conv_q imply.  Every hand-rolled
conversion step disappears if the gate consumes this.

STRATA_BAD_CONV_FIXTURE=1 makes the writer emit the conv weights in the wrong order while the expected outputs were
still computed with the right one - the known-bad case, built into the reference rather than reconstructed by hand.
Its comment says why the flip has to live in the writer and not the loader: "wrong against wrong ... and the gate
would pass - which is exactly how the original defect survived every gate."

HOW TO CONSUME IT WITHOUT TOUCHING kda_gate: split the raw file into the 29-file directory layout the gate already
reads.  All array sizes follow from the header and the known shapes - attn_norm 4096, wq/wk/wv 8192x4096,
conv_q/k/v 4x8192, ssm_a and dt_bias 64 and 128 respectively (measure which, do not assume), ssm_f_a/g_a 128,
ssm_f_b/g_b 8192, ssm_beta 64, o_norm 128, wo 4096x8192, x tokensx4096, qc/kc/vc/o tokensx8192, g/q/k/v/attn
tokensx8192, beta tokensx64, result tokensx4096.  A splitter should ASSERT that the sizes it assigns consume the
file exactly - that turns the one remaining unknown (dt_bias's length) into a check rather than a guess, and a
mismatch is a hard error rather than a silently shifted read.

THEN: build block 1's fixture from the oracle's raw output, run kda_gate, and the question of whether block 1's
kernel path is correct gets answered by data that cannot be self-inconsistent.  That is the next action.

########  BLOCK 1 PASSES - THE KERNEL WAS CORRECT ALL ALONG, AND THE FIXTURE WAS THE DEFECT  ########

Against a fixture produced by the oracle's own --raw-fixture writer and split faithfully into the layout the gate
reads (layout verified: 551273496 bytes = 24 + 4*137818368, exact):

    xn    PASS  5.254e-08        g     PASS  7.706e-07        v     PASS  1.033e-06
    qc    PASS  1.358e-06        beta  PASS  6.999e-07        attn  PASS  1.313e-06
    kc    PASS  1.484e-06        q     PASS  1.478e-06        o     PASS  1.143e-06
    vc    PASS  1.033e-06        k     PASS  1.244e-06
    KDA GATE: PASS

qc, kc and vc were failing by a factor of about one against the hand-rolled fixture.  Against reference output that
is self-consistent by construction they pass at roughly 1e-06.  The kernel was never wrong; the previous fixture's
inter_qc did not follow from that fixture's own xn, wq and conv_q, and no gate over it could have said anything
either way.  This is the fourth independent confirmation that the engine is correct on this path - the others being
the four-significant-digit match of the engine's qc to the inconsistent fixture's, the conv convention's own measured
0.02%-vs-90.56%, and block 0's 12/12.

xn PASSING at 5.254e-08 also validates the one non-copy step in the splitter: the writer emits x but not xn, so xn is
derived as rms_norm(x, attn_norm) there, and the engine agrees with it.  A derivation that had been subtly wrong
would have shown up here rather than being assumed.

WHAT MADE THIS WORK, in the order it mattered:
  1. recomputing the reference from the reference's own inputs - three lines of numpy that localised the problem to
     the fixture and exonerated the kernel, after two rounds of narrowing had looked at kernel code;
  2. using the oracle's own writer instead of hand-rolling a conversion, because that writer emits exactly the arrays
     the loader handed kda_block and that call's return, so no step can introduce an inconsistency;
  3. deriving sizes from the header AND asserting the aggregate consumes the file - which caught five wrong sizes
     (ssm_f_a, ssm_f_b, ssm_beta, ssm_g_a, ssm_g_b were 128x to 4096x larger than the dimensions suggested) before a
     single byte was written, rather than producing a fifth inconsistent fixture;
  4. not trusting a reshape of a foreign in-memory array, which is what made the first self-consistency assertion
     fire wrongly on block 0.

TOOLS ADDED: tools/split_raw_fixture.py (raw fixture -> the 29-file layout, with the aggregate size assertion).
FIXTURES: /home/peb/moredata/glm5-kda-l1-oracle (oracle-produced, block 1, PASS),
/home/peb/moredata/glm5-kda-l0-t1 (block 0, 12/12 PASS), /home/peb/moredata/glm5-kda-badconv (known-bad, must FAIL).

########  BLOCKS 0, 1 AND 2 ALL PASS 12/12 - EVERY LEADING-DENSE KDA BLOCK IS VERIFIED  ########

    block 1   KDA GATE: PASS   (12/12 now that result.bin exists; the stage list was skipping it by name)
    block 2   result 2.982e-06  xn 1.009e-07  qc 1.985e-06  kc 1.187e-06  vc 9.338e-07
              g 7.893e-07  beta 2.675e-07  q 1.373e-06  k 1.198e-06  v 9.338e-07
              attn 2.145e-06  o 2.243e-06                       KDA GATE: PASS

leading_dense_block_count is 3, so blocks 0, 1 and 2 are the KDA+dense kind and all three now pass every stage the
gate compares, including the block output.  The next KDA block would be 4, where the block kind changes.

THE PIPELINE IS NOW THREE COMMANDS PER BLOCK and involves no hand-rolled conversion:
    glm5_kda_reference.py --gguf GGUF --layer N --tokens T --raw-fixture RAW
    split_raw_fixture.py RAW OUTDIR
    kda_gate OUTDIR T
Both split and gate verify their own inputs on the way - the splitter asserts the array sizes consume the file
exactly, and the gate reports per-stage relative error against the fixture.  That is what makes a third block cheap
rather than another investigation.

SCOPE, stated precisely so this is not overclaimed.  What is verified: the KDA stage, on the real weights of blocks
0, 1 and 2, against reference output that is self-consistent by construction, at T=1 (and T=5 for block 0).  What is
NOT thereby verified: the FFN and hyper-connection stages of blocks 1 and 2 - those are verified for block 0 only -
and the chain itself, because the gate's inputs come from the oracle's seed rather than from block N-1's output.  A
passing KDA gate says the kernel handles this block's weights correctly, which is what it is for; it does not say
the block computes the right answer from the real hidden state.

FIXTURES: glm5-kda-l0-t1 (block 0, 12/12), glm5-kda-l1-oracle (block 1, 12/12), glm5-kda-l2-oracle (block 2, 12/12),
glm5-kda-l0-t5 (block 0 at T=5), glm5-kda-badconv (known-bad, must FAIL).  Tools: split_raw_fixture.py,
make_kda_fixture.py (superseded - keep for reference, prefer the oracle's writer).

########  BLOCK 4 PASSES 12/12 ACROSS A BLOCK-KIND BOUNDARY  ########

    result 2.783e-06   xn 8.791e-08   qc 1.074e-06   kc 1.073e-06   vc 2.555e-06
    g 6.950e-07   beta 7.876e-07   q 1.360e-06   k 1.529e-06   v 2.555e-06
    attn 2.415e-06   o 2.023e-06                        KDA GATE: PASS

Blocks 0, 1, 2 and 4 now pass every stage the gate compares - four blocks, including one beyond
leading_dense_block_count=3, so the KDA path is verified across the boundary where the block kind changes from
dense to MoE.  Block 3 is of a different attention kind (MLA) and is not covered by this gate at all.

The splitter's aggregate check reported the SAME total, 551273496 bytes, for blocks 1, 2 and 4, and passed for all
three.  Since the table is measured rather than derived and the check requires the sizes to consume the file
exactly, that identical figure is positive evidence the KDA geometry is uniform across the model - and if any block
had differed, the check would have refused rather than produced a misaligned read.  That is the property the
hand-rolled fixtures never had.

METHOD, now settled and worth reusing verbatim for any further block:
    glm5_kda_reference.py --gguf GGUF --layer N --tokens T --raw-fixture RAW
    split_raw_fixture.py RAW OUTDIR
    kda_gate OUTDIR T
Three commands, no hand-rolled conversion, and both tools verify their inputs on the way.  Blocks 0-2 cost one
investigation each to establish and block 4 cost one command.

SCOPE, unchanged: the KDA stage on these blocks' real weights, at T=1.  The FFN and hyper-connection stages are
